   1|                                                                              In Memoríam
   2|                                            Lillian S. Griffiths
   3|                       Lillian passed away on September 30th at the age of 99. Lillian grew up on the family farm in
   4|                       Lunenburg with four brothers and five sisters. After distinguishing herself as a scholar-athlete at
   5|                       Lunenburg High and graduating from Fitchburg State in 1941, she began her 33-year career in
   6|                       education as a junior high teacher in Lunenburg. She went on to teach fifth grade, earn her
   7|                       Master's Degree from Boston University. and serve as the Reading Supervisor for grades K-8
   8|                       from 1957 until her retirement in 1977. She was past president of the Lunenburg Teachers'
   9|                       Association and the North Worcester County's Council on Reading. She was an avid golfer and
  10|                       past president of the Oak Hill Country Club Women's Golf Association. She also coached
  11|                       basketball and field hockey at Lunenburg High and was deeply honored to be one of the first ten
  12|                       inductees to the LHS Athletic Hall of Fame in 2007.
  13|                                   Walter Barrett Keeler, age 90, died April 24th. Mr. Keeler was active in the
  14|                       Lunenburg community, taking leadership positions in the United Parish; serving on the School
  15|                       Committee from 1965-1974; helping to found Hickory Hills Landowners Inc. in 1978 which
  16|                       purchased the lake for residents and shareholders; working tirelessly to have Hickory Hills roads
  17|                       taken over by the Town; and culminating in service on the Lunenburg Board of Selectmen from
  18|                       1984-1987.
  19|                       Sarah Powell, 103, passed away on September 13, 2017. She was the recipient of the Boston
  20|                       Post Cane Award in 2015. At that time, she said she was ancient. Sarah's wit will be missed by
  21|                       all, as well as her special ability to say the alphabet backwards!
  22|                       Einard Warpula, 89, passed away on May 17th at home surrounded by family. Einard lived on
  23|                       the same property on Flat Hill Road in Lunenburg for 87 years. Einard owned Warpula Bus
  24|                       Company for 25 years, safely transporting thousands of students in Lunenburg. Upon "retiring,"
  25|                       he drove motor-coaches for Wilson Bus Lines and school buses for local contractors, right up
  26|                       until last December. He was a wealth of knowledge on best-routes, landmarks, parking spots and
  27|                       all things mechanical. Einard loved to drive and trained many others to drive as well. He loved a
  28|                       good story and a good laugh and always found something positive in every situation.
  29|                                                                                                2

===PAGE 5=== (ocr, 34 lines)
   1|                                                                        LUNENBURG PROFILE
   2|                                                                        YEAR INCORPORATED- 1728
   3|                                                                            FY'17 TAX RATE-$19.98
   4|                             OPEN TOWN MEETING GOVERNMENT- FIVE MEMBER BOARD OF SELECTMEN
   5|                                                     ANNUAL TOWN MEETING- IST SATURDAY IN MAY
   6|                                                    ANNUAL TOWN ELECTION- 3RD SATURDAY IN MAY
   7|                                                                        AREA- 26.63 SQUARE MILES
   8|                                                                          ROAD MILES- ABOUT 100
   9|                         Lunenburg was settled in 1718 and incorporated in 1728. It occupies a land area of
  10|                         approximately 26.4 square miles. Lunenburg is located in north central Massachusetts
  11|                         approximately 43 miles from Boston. It is bordered on the west by the Town of Ashby and the
  12|                         City of Fitchburg, on the south by the Town of Lancaster and the City of Leominster, on the east
  13|                         by the Town of Shirley, and on the north by the Town of Townsend
  14|                         Local legislative decisions are made by an open town meeting consisting of all registered voters
  15|                         in the town. Subject to the legislative decisions made by town meeting, the affairs of the town
  16|                         are generally administered by a five member Board of Selectmen and a Town Manager. Local
  17|                         school affairs are administered by an elected seven member School Committee while local taxes
  18|                         are assessed by a three member Board of Assessors, all are appointed on an at-large basis for
  19|                         staggered three-year terms.
  20|                         Lunenburg is a semi-rural community located in northern Worcester County. Three water bodies
  21|                         account for somewhat of a clustered approach to the town's historical development. Lake
  22|                         Whalom and the former Whalom Amusement Park delineated the Whalom District from the
  23|                         remainder of Lunenburg. This district was developed as a housing base for many factory
  24|                         workers working in the mills and factories of Leominster and Fitchburg. In many ways the
  25|                         characteristics of the Whalom District more closely reflect characteristics of these cities than of
  26|                         the rest of Lunenburg. The Hickory Hills area of town, around Hickory Hills Lake, was
  27|                         developed as a private community comprised largely of vacation cottages. The third body of
  28|                         water is Shirley Reservoir, a public water body also developed with mainly vacation cottages
  29|                         that have now been modified to year-round residences. Commercial and industrial development
  30|                         is permitted in several areas of town, primarily along the two major arteries (Routes 2A and 13),
  31|                         and the town's peripheries, due largely to the close proximity of Leominster's and Fitchburg's
  32|                         sewer and water systems. Although many farms and orchards had slowly developed into single
  33|                         family housing, farming continues on in Lunenburg. At the Annual Town Meeting on May 4"
  34|                         2013, the Town voted to establish an Agricultural Commission to represent, preserve, and enhance farming in the community and to administer the "Right to Farm" bylaw.

===PAGE 15=== (ocr, 2 lines)
   1|                                                               ADMINISTRATION
   2|                                                                                               15

===PAGE 16=== (ocr, 38 lines)
   1|                                                                                 REPORT OF THE
   2|                                                                                TOWN MANAGER
   3|                       The calendar year of 2017 was another year of progress for the Board of Selectmen and Town
   4|                       Manager's Office. During 2017 there were many topics addressed and accomplishments to
   5|                       highlight:
   6|                            • On January 17".             ", the new Town Manager, Heather Lemieux, began. During this month
   7|                                  the Town Manager pursued entering the Commonwealth's Community Compact Program
   8|                                  to implement up to three best practices. By entering the program, the State would
   9|                                   provide grant funds to implement these best practices and it would also enable the Town
  10|                                  to be eligible for an Information Technology Grant and a Regionalization/Efficiency
  11|                                   Grant. At the Board's first meeting in February the Selectmen chose three best practices,
  12|                                  including to "Implement a Citizen Engagement Co-Creation Plan", to "Implement
  13|                                   Stormwater Management Measures",                      ', and to "Implement a Cyber-Security Training
  14|                                   Program". The Town entered the program in February and a ceremony was held with
  15|                                   Lieutenant Governor Karyn Polito on May 4*' to sign the compact. All three best
  16|                                   practices are expected to be completed by June of 2018.
  17|                            • Other noteworthy items that happened in February include a Roads Workshop that was
  18|                                  held to review the list of roads that would be paved for the ensuing year as part of the
  19|                                   $4M debt exclusion for a pavement management plan, as well as a discussion on entering
  20|                                  the Complete Streets Program under Massachusetts Department of Transportation that
  21|                                   would improve accessibility and safety of travel modes for all ages and abilities. Town
  22|                                   Manager Heather Lemieux, Selectmen Chairman Jamie Toale, Land Use Director Adam
  23|                                   Burney and DPW Director Jack Rodriquenz formulated a Complete Streets policy that
  24|                                   was approved by the Board in April of 2017. The policy was submitted to MassDOT and
  25|                                  received a favorable scoring that enabled the Town to receive $37,000 in grant funds to
  26|                                  hire a consultant to assist the Town to put together a list of projects into a Prioritization
  27|                                  Plan. Once completed this would be submitted to MassDOT and the Town would be
  28|                                   eligible to receive up to $400,000 in construction funds to complete projects on the list.
  29|                                  It is estimated the Prioritization Plan will be completed by May of 2018. Also in
  30|                                   February the Selectmen interviewed nine law firms and in April selected Mead, Talerman
  31|                                   & Costa as the Town's new Town Counsel, who took over from KP Law in July. The
  32|                                   Town Manager presented a balanced budget for Fiscal Year 2018 to the Finance
  33|                                   Committee and Selectmen in mid-February for a total of $39,781,168. Part of the Town
  34|                                   Manager's recommendation was to begin to fund the Other Post-Employment Benefits
  35|                                   Trust Fund for the unfunded liability of retiree health insurance in the amount of
  36|                                   $100,000 and to create a Special Purpose Stabilization Account for known large capital
  37|                                  items such as fire trucks with a starting appropriation of $200,000.
  38|                                                                                               16

===PAGE 17=== (ocr, 38 lines)
   1|                             • During the month of March the Town received notification that the Regionalization and
   2|                                   Efficiency Grant application for a feasibility and architectural design of a Regional
   3|                                   Animal Control facility was awarded to Lunenburg, Fitchburg, and Townsend in the
   4|                                  amount of $42,257. Fitchburg later backed out due to privatization of their animal
   5|                                   control services, leaving Lunenburg as the lead community. The expected completion of
   6|                                   a design is by June 2018.
   7|                             • The Town received an Indoor Air Quality Report for Town Hall based on testing done by
   8|                                  the Department of Public Health in the fall of 2016. There were a number of
   9|                                  recommendations made including remediation of the mold in the Town Hall basement,
  10|                                  seal the basement to eliminate any future moisture problems, clean bird waste in the attic,
  11|                                  and clean the HVAC system. A portion of these were accomplished during FY2017
  12|                                  using funds from the Town Facilities account and a Reserve Fund Transfer authorized by
  13|                                  the Finance Committee. The remainder of the funding was appropriated at the Special
  14|                                   Town Meeting on November 28" and all projects are expected to be complete by the end
  15|                                   of Fiscal Year 2018.
  16|                            • The Summer Street redevelopment and roadway improvement project continued to move
  17|                                  forward with a slated beginning of 2018. The estimated $1OM costs for this project
  18|                                   which includes Lunenburg, Leominster and Fitchburg, will be funded through the
  19|                                   Regional Transportation Improvement Plan (TIP), which are state and federal
  20|                                   transportation funds.
  21|                            • The Right of Way process was finalized by the Town in August and met the deadline
  22|                                   MassDOT's deadline to advertise the project at the end of August.
  23|                            • In May the Selectmen approved an Entertainment License at 300 Holman Street after
  24|                                  receiving input from all relevant public safety and public health departments, as well as
  25|                                  residents at the public hearing. The Annual Town Meeting on May 6' passed a balanced
  26|                                  budget for FY 2018 with an omnibus budget of $35,690,393.70. Amendments to the FY
  27|                                  2018 budget were made at a Special Town Meeting in November for a revised total of
  28|                                   $35,855,365.70.
  29|                             • The Board held a Tax Title Workshop in May to review the list of properties provided by
  30|                                  the Treasurer/Collector and discuss properties that the Conservation Commission and
  31|                                   Selectmen were interested in foreclosing as they abutted existing conservation land. The
  32|                                   Selectmen agreed on a finalized list of properties to proceed with a Tax Title Assignment
  33|                                   Auction and the remainder of the properties to proceed through the foreclosure process.
  34|                             • The Selectmen and the Town Manager made their annual appointments in June for FY
  35|                                  2018. The Selectmen's workshop in June was to review the Traffic Rules & Regulations
  36|                                   with Counsel Joel Bard and Police Chief James Marino which became finalized in
  37|                                   September 2017.
  38|                                                                                               17

===PAGE 18=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|                             • In June the Town was notified that the Information Technology grant the Town Manager
   2|                                   submitted was awarded in the amount of $40,000 to purchase electronic permitting
   3|                                   software utilized by various town departments. After a selection process, the Town chose
   4|                                   Viewpoint. The e-permitting software will create greater efficiencies both internally and
   5|                                   externally for users that apply for various permits and licenses as they will be able to log
   6|                                  in remotely, conduct business, and check the status of their application. The software is
   7|                                   expected to go "live" for external users in late May 2018.
   8|                            • In July the Town Manager presented various updates to the Board's Policies and
   9|                                   Procedures Manual including personnel policies, a new social media policy and revised
  10|                                   financial policies recommended by the Finance Committee. Also in July, the Selectmen
  11|                                   made appointments to the Board of Health and Planning Board to fill vacancies until the
  12|                                  next annual election and re-appointed the Animal Control Officer.
  13|                            • The development of a plan for town facilities, specifically the Town Hall, the Ritter
  14|                                   Building, the old Primary, the Brooks House, and the Passios continued to be a consistent
  15|                                  topic at many Selectmen meetings, the Annual Town Meeting and the Special Town
  16|                                   Meeting. In August the Board held a workshop to discuss town facilities, their current
  17|                                  and future use. At that meeting the Board voted to move forward with the concept of
  18|                                  consolidating town offices under one roof at the Passios Building. A public forum was
  19|                                  held on December 7* to receive input from the public on the current and future use of
  20|                                  town facilities.
  21|                            • The Town auctioned off five properties that were town owned due to foreclosure on
  22|                                   August 29 receiving a total of $37,000 that will positively impact the Town's FY19 Free
  23|                                   Cash. The Town Manager presented a change to the Pay-As-You Throw program that
  24|                                  included changing to a Retail Store Distribution model where the bag vendor delivers the
  25|                                  bags directly to the stores that distribute them, added an Extra Large bag, increased the
  26|                                  thickness of the Large bag, and changed from a twist tie to drawstring bag.
  27|                            • A Five Year Moratorium for Road Openings policy presented by the DPW Director was
  28|                                  adopted by the Board in September with an associated fee schedule that was approved in
  29|                                   November. The September workshop was a joint meeting with the Planning Board on
  30|                                   Economic Development to discuss the work that has been done by the Town in the past
  31|                                   on economic development, types and level of economic development and next steps. The
  32|                                   Selectmen held another economic development workshop in December to include the
  33|                                   Sewer Commission, Planning Board, and the North Central Chamber of Commerce. The
  34|                                   Town Manager applied for and received District Local Technical Assistance that
  35|                                   provided consultant services from Montachusett Regional Planning Commission to
  36|                                  facilitate meetings between the Town and business community that would focus on
  37|                                   promoting the local economy. There were a total of three meetings with residents and
  38|                                   business owners and MRPC presented their written report with recommendations at a
  39|                                   Selectmen's meeting in December.
  40|                                                                                               18

===PAGE 19=== (ocr, 44 lines)
   1|                             • After conducting an online survey to solicit the input on the color of Town Hall (the current
   2|                                   brown color or white), over 600 respondents voted, with a majority choosing white. The project
   3|                                   was put out to bid in September and bids were received in October. The total cost for the project
   4|                                   came in at budget of $88,000 using funds that were approved through a previous town meeting
   5|                                   capital planning article. The Town Manager announced the award of a Department of Energy
   6|                                   Resources Technical Assistance grant in the amount of $5,000 for grant administration services
   7|                                  for the Green Communities to prepare a competitive grant application to be submitted in March
   8|                                  of 2018.
   9|                             • The Board conducted a series of liquor license violation hearings in October, a workshop with
  10|                                   town boards/committees/commissions to review accomplishments and goals, as well as how the
  11|                                   Selectmen could support them.
  12|                             • The Special Town Meeting on November 28* included a number of articles such as amending the
  13|                                  FY 2018 budget to capture additional tax growth in both real estate and personal property, a
  14|                                  citizen's petition to supplement funding approved at previous town meeting to improve Wallis
  15|                                  Park, additional revolving fund accounts, fund the first year of the Firefighter and Clerical Union
  16|                                   Agreements, funding to perform property condition assessments for 6 town and 1 school building,
  17|                                   funding for a conceptual design for town offices at the Passios building, acceptance of certain
  18|                                   Massachusetts General Laws, authorization for a telecommunications lease at Town Hall for up to
  19|                                  25 years, acceptance of land for conservation at 842R Chase Road, and an amendment to the
  20|                                   Zoning Bylaw.
  21|                             • The Board approved all the annual liquor licenses, common victualler, weekday entertainment,
  22|                                   automatic amusement, jukebox, and motor vehicle licenses.
  23|                       After just over a year in the position, Lunenburg has become my home away from home. The residents of
  24|                       Lunenburg demonstrate the true meaning of community that I have witnessed in various acts, whether it
  25|                       be supporting our local businesses, non-profit groups raising funds for those in need, or words of
  26|                       gratitude for our public safety and public works departments. I have the privilege of working with many
  27|                       talented individuals that are committed to helping the residents and business owners of Lunenburg and are
  28|                       consistently looking for ways to better serve the public. The accomplishments noted above, among the
  29|                       numerous day-to-day responsibilities, could not happen without the cooperation of dedicated staff,
  30|                       appointed, and elected officials that make the wheels of town government turn. Thank you to the Board
  31|                       of Selectmen for the level of respect that each of you gives your fellow members, the staff, and the public
  32|                       and well thought out decisions. Thank you to my Executive Assistant, Elaine Peterson, for all your hard
  33|                       work and keeping the office running smoothly. I would also like to thank the committee and board
  34|                       members, department heads and staff for the high level of service they provide to the residents of town.
  35|                       Specifically, I would like to express gratitude to the department heads that lead our various departments:
  36|                       James Marino, Police Chief; Patrick Sullivan, Fire Chief; Jack Rodriquenz, DPW Director; Karen
  37|                       Brochu, Town Accountant/Finance Director; Myleen Mallari, Treasurer/Collector; Louise Paquette,
  38|                       Assessors; Steve Malandrinos, IT Director; Adam Burney, Land Use Director; Sue Doherty, COA
  39|                       Director; Martha Moore, Library Director; Kathy Herrick, Town Clerk; and all their staff for their talent
  40|                       and working together to make our town run as efficiently as possible. Lastly, thank you to the residents
  41|                       and businesses that make Lunenburg a wonderful place to visit, work, and live.
  42|                       Respectfully submitted,
  43|                       Heather R. Lemieux
  44|                                                                                               19

===PAGE 48=== (ocr, 33 lines)
   1|                                     ARCHITECTURAL PRESERVATION DISTRICT COMMISSION
   2|                                                                                          (APDC)
   3|                           In 2017, the Lunenburg APDC held regular monthly meetings, as well as public hearings:
   4|                           both on its own and in conjunction with the Board of Selectmen. The main focus of work
   5|                           was to draft guidelines for implementation of the Architectural Preservation District bylaw
   6|                           and amend them so that they were acceptable to the Board of Selectmen. In November, after
   7|                           a vote of approval from the Board of Selectmen, the APDC voted to adopt the amended
   8|                           guidelines and submitted then to the Town Clerk, making the Architectural Preservation
   9|                           District bylaw fully operational.
  10|                                                                            BUILDING DEPARTMENT
  11|                           The Building Department is located on the second floor of the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massa-
  12|                           chusetts Avenue. Office hours for the Building Department are Monday and Wednesday 8:00 A.M.
  13|                           to 4:00 P.M., Tuesday and Thursday 8:00 A.M. until 6:00 P.M. The Department is closed on Friday.
  14|                           Garry Rhodes serves as Building Commissioner, Andrew Hudson serves as the Assistant Building
  15|                           Inspector and Lisa Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Building Department.
  16|                           Lisa Normandin also serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Zoning Board of Appeals.
  17|                           Building Permits for 2017 have increased from 500 in 2016 to 538. Solar permits continue to be
  18|                           strong. We continue to see permits for weatherization which can be contributed to support provided
  19|                           by "Mass Save." In many cases Mass Save provides financial support to homeowners who upgrade
  20|                           insulation and do air sealing.
  21|                           The state of Massachusetts, as of January 1s' 2018, has implemented the 9t Edition of the Building
  22|                           Code, CMR 780. The changes will impact both residential and commercial construction. The ninth
  23|                           edition is based on 2015 International Building Code.
  24|                           We receive many inquiries from residents concerning sheds. You only need a building permit when
  25|                           the shed is over 200 square teet. Even though a building permit may not be required the shed must
  26|                           comply with Lunenburg Zoning Bylaw for location. The side and rear yard setback varies from 5
  27|                           feet to 15 feet depending on the zoning district. You may also be subject to setbacks for both Con-
  28|                           servation (wetlands) and Board of Health (septic systems).
  29|                           Building Permits have been issued for all 120 apartment units known as Whalom Luxury Apartments
  30|                           located off Whalom Road. Occupancy permits have been issued for the first building.
  31|                           The forms to apply for permits can be obtained on-line at www.lunenburgma.gov or by stopping by
  32|                           at 960 Massachusetts Avenue during normal business hours.
  33|                                                                                               48

===PAGE 49=== (ocr, 27 lines)
   1|                                                                      CEMETERY COMMISSION
   2|                          The Cemetery Commission had a busy 2017, as the installation of the Niche Wall was com-
   3|                          pleted in the North Cemetery, while paving and tree pruning was completed in the South
   4|                           Cemetery. The Lunenburg Cemetery Commission continues to plan for the future, as the
   5|                          Commission oversaw an additional 20 services that included burials and cremations.
   6|                          In planning for future growth, the Cemetery Commission has begun to do so, with the addi-
   7|                          tion of a completed Niche Wall that adds to the availability of planned space and offers a
   8|                          modern and updated way for families to make additional choices for their loved ones.
   9|                          Lunenburg Boy Scout Troop 1728, through their Scoutmaster Jim Parker, Scout Michael
  10|                          Clark and his father Mike Clark, together with other Scouts and their parents, completed the
  11|                          North Cemetery Flag Pole Eagle Scout Project and did a great job!
  12|                          Commissioners Lance D. May, Lorraine Mendoza and William Tyler, would like to thank
  13|                          the Lunenburg Lions Club for their donation of 2 Colorado Blue Spruce trees for the North
  14|                          Cemetery, along with Robert Ullo for assisting with the plowing of snow from the entrance
  15|                          to the South Cemetery. The Commission would also like to thank Linda Shea for donating
  16|                          and maintaining flowers at the South Cemetery gate and Erik Locke and the inmates from
  17|                          the Worcester County Sheriff's Community Service Program for their assistance during the
  18|                          Summer and Fall months on clean-ups and maintenance of both cemeteries. Also, thanks to
  19|                          Dave Rodgers as well, for donating water and Gator-Ade for the inmates.
  20|                          The Cemetery Commission maintains an on-line mapping system for both the North and
  21|                          South Cemeteries that can be found at http://www.map.ramaker.com/ecims/.
  22|                          The Commissioners would also like to offer their "special thanks" to Cemetery
  23|                          Superintendent Todd Holman for his assistance, efforts and help during this busy year.
  24|                          Lance D May, Chairman
  25|                          Lorraine Mendoza, Vice-Chairman
  26|                           William Tyler, Clerk

===PAGE 51=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|                                                                           CONSERVATION COMMISSION
   2|                        The Town of Lunenburg Conservation Commission is a seven member commission, appointed to 3 year terms by
   3|                        the Board of Selectmen, whose duties are codified under the Conservation Commission Act MGL chapter 40 s 8C.
   4|                        The Commission enforces the Massachusetts Wetland Protection Act MGL chapter 131 s 40 and Article XXII of
   5|                        the local bylaws for the protection of wetlands.
   6|                         The year 2017 was, as last year, one of a very busy and active agenda containing both public hearings and
   7|                         meetings for a wide range of projects from commercial development, subdivisions and small single family up-
   8|                        keep projects and a large scale solar proposal. The Conservation Commission worked to spell out goals of prepar-
   9|                        ing to use the year 2018 to complete revising its regulations to provide consistency with the original by-law and
  10|                        to find those sections that may be now out of date and need to be modified. This effort has been methodically
  11|                        approached and very comprehensive and is estimated to be completed by summer of 2018.
  12|                        The Commission also held numerous inspections with the commission administrator on weekends prior to
  13|                        meetings to familiarize themselves with the upcoming proposals that would appear on their agenda. The Com-
  14|                        mission continues to work with the town on familiarizing itself with the upcoming storm water mandates from
  15|                        the EPA and working towards compliance both by procedure and with any necessary codification.
  16|                        The Conservation Commission completed forest stewardship planning after receiving grants (see table on page
  17|                        2). These stewardship plans for five individual parcels, will aid the commission in implementing environmental
  18|                        management plans which include timber stand management. This December, the Commission bid and initiated
  19|                        timber and environmental management in the small town forest. Other Conservation areas plans have been
  20|                        completed for are the Lane property, The Large and Small Town Forests, the open space on Robbs Hill and the
  21|                        Cowdery. The Commission retained Gary Gouldrup, a noted forester with many years of experience working in
  22|                        town for the commission and private landowners. The Conservation Commission also held public recreational
  23|                        walks through its open space parcels and plans to continue to do so.
  24|                        The Commission has also worked closely with Lake Shirley and Hickory Hills in the implementations of their man-
  25|                        agement plans becoming actively involved in continuation of formulating updates designed to give a better pic-
  26|                        ture of the overall health of the ecosystems and the various ways to ensure continued protection of these valua-
  27|                        ble resources.

  29|                        in the matters of trails and open space management. We wish Tom well in future endeavors and are grateful for
  30|                        both his previous service and his willingness to continue to contribute! The Commission would like to take this
  31|                        time to welcome Catie Childs as our newest commissioner. Catie, a former member of a Conservation Commis-
  32|                        sion in another community she previously resided, presented the Commission with a qualified candidate who
  33|                        could start off the ground running. We welcome and value her presence as the newest member of the Lunen-
  34|                        burg Conservation Commission!

   1|                                                                            COUNCIL ON AGING
   2|                          The mission of the Council on Aging is to advocate for the improved quality of life for all seniors through
   3|                          supportive services, programs and education. Councils on Aging were established under Massachusetts Gen-
   4|                          eral Law Chapter 40, § 8B, to include setting policy for the administration of elder programs or services and to
   5|                          develop, coordinate and conduct such activities which advocate for elder issues.
   6|                          The Council on Aging staff consisted of Susan Doherty, Director; Amanda Koeck, Administrative Assistant
   7|                          and Transportation; Faith A. Anderson, Outreach Coordinator; Joanne Brazell, Meal Site Coordinator; Judy
   8|                           Alario and Elsa Watson, Meal Site Assistants; Jim McGuigan and Paul Grunditz, MART Van Drivers.
   9|                          The Council on Aging Board is an 11 member board appointed to 3 year terms by the Board of Selectmen.
  10|                          The COA Board members were; Chairperson Pete Lincoln, Vice-Chairperson Jacquelyn Dwyer, Betty DiGia-
  11|                          como,Sarah Grant, Brian Guenard, Deb Lincoln, Kevin McNally, Cheryl Moisan, Diane Nowd, Jane Rabbitt,
  12|                          and Judith Tarbell.
  13|                          The Town budget alone does not support this senior center. Other sources of funding are the Eagle House Sup-
  14|                          porters Inc., a non-profit 501(c)3 group, Formula Grant funding from the Commonwealth of Massachusetts
  15|                          and Montachusett Regional Transit Authority. We are very lucky to have a large volunteer force at the Eagle
  16|                          House. In FY17 we had 41 volunteers who contributed 2,218.5 hours of their time to help us doing things
  17|                          such as driving for the Meals on Wheels Program, program leaders, kitchen helpers, office help, library, and
  18|                          friendly visitors. We greatly appreciate their service.
  19|                          In July of FY17, the Eagle House Senior Community Center re-opened again on Fridays for business. This
  20|                          paved the way for more programming and transportation opportunities for our residents.
  21|                          The Eagle House offers a variety of programs, such as health and wellness, arts and crafts, intergenerational,
  22|                          bocce, cards and games, cultural, information and referral, and social events both at the senior center and
  23|                          offsite. In FY17 we offered 21 new programs which included various speakers and exercise programs.
  24|                          There were approximately 906 people who utilized the senior center in FY17. Those 906 people compiled
  25|                          25,986 units of duplicated service by participating in the programs listed above.
  26|                          In FY17 the Council on Aging delivered 4,183 Meals on Wheels to 48 clients and provided 3,117 rides to 64
  27|                          clients on our senior van.
  28|                          We are very fortunate to have a Town Government that supports our center and understands the importance of
  29|                          supporting the Council on Aging to maintain quality services for our elders. Special thanks go to our 501(c)3
  30|                          fundraising group, Eagle House Supporters Inc. who donated $9,214 for equipment and programs at the Eagle
  31|                          House Senior Community Center. Also special thanks to all of our outside agencies for donating time and
  32|                          programming for our clients.
  33|                          The Council on Aging newsletter lists current events and is available for you to pick up at the Eagle House
  34|                          Senior Community Center, Town Hall, Library, Post Office, local churches and various businesses throughout
  35|                          the community.

===PAGE 55=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|                                                                    DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   2|                                    The Department of Public Works combines the resources of the Highway, Park, Cemetery, Sewer, and
   3|                         Facilities divisions, and Vehicle Maintenance services into one department organizing and scheduling personnel
   4|                         and equipment to best serve the residents of the community through the effective delivery of an extensive scope
   5|                         of services with a high degree of efficiency.
   6|                         DEPARTMENTAL OBJECTIVES
   7|                                    "Public Works is either working or on call twenty-four hours a day three hundred and sixty-five days a
   8|                         year to respond to any weather, infrastructure, roadway, or facility related emergency that may arise."
   9|                                     We strive to enhance the awareness of the value of Public Works provides to our Town, and ensure oth-
  10|                         ers are aware of our contribution. This is not for an expectation of praise, but rather to make sure efforts are not
  11|                         forgotten as they merge into the fabric of our everyday expectation of service.
  12|                                    Major events, particularly in the form of devastating storms, over the past years have highlighted the fact
  13|                         that Public Works has a seat at the table and is a major player in emergency management. Town Officials, Police,
  14|                         and Fire acknowledge that Public Works is critical in order to facilitate the required services of others. We must
  15|                         continue to build on this awareness.
  16|                                    This department must also reach out to the public we serve and educate them to the fact that the common
  17|                         services they receive do not just happen, but rather are the efforts of a dedicated staff of individuals, service com-
  18|                         panies, and vendors. Safe roadways, wastewater control, solid waste collection and recycling operations, tree re-
  19|                         moval, storm water management facilities and grounds maintenance, winter snow and ice removal, cemeteries
  20|                         and parks are the expected services supporting everyone in the community.
  21|                                     We will continue to engage the public in order to set reasonable expectations and gather support for an-
  22|                         nual budget requests, capital requests, and specific projects.
  23|                         PAVEMENT MANAGEMENT
  24|                                    Historically the amount of funding dedicated to roadway paving and paving related services has been
  25|                         grossly inadequate resulting in a negative pavement condition index. It became a high priority goal of the BOS as
  26|                         Road Commissioners, to achieve a pavement condition of 72%-75% Town-wide. Although State funded Chapter
  27|                         90 funds remained stagnant, the dedicated Pavement Management line in the operational budget grew substantial-
  28|                        ly.
  29|                                     Results of the newly revised Comprehensive Pavement Management Plan displayed clearly the inequity
  30|                         of the distribution of paving funds toward higher use main and collector roads leaving local neighborhoods unat-
  31|                        tended. Using the PMP as evidence, residents went to the polls to vote favorably on a four-million-dollar debt
  32|                         exclusion targeting exclusively the local roads. Expending approximately a million dollars in the initial year re-
  33|                         sulted in a major enhancement of road conditions.
  34|                                    The debt exclusion money combined with state and operational funds brought the total paving value to
  35|                        nearly two million dollars in the first construction season as we attacked the nine-million-dollar backlog of re-
  36|                         pairs.
  37|                                     Our pothole log has and is expected to lessen exponentially as new paving increases, a clear benefit of
  38|                         the dollars expended. There is much work yet to do since we are at the very beginning of the program but expec-
  39|                        tations are high.
  40|                                     The seasonal crack sealing program has been effective in some locations where applicable however this
  41|                         protocol is designed as a temporary solution with an effective life span of around five years. The mechanics of
  42|                        the process involves the installation of rubberized asphalt under high pressure into cracks or separations in the
  43|                        pavement. The Pavement Management Plan identifies specific areas where this method is the best option. This
  44|                        application does not take the place of curb to curb paving, only postpones it and is not applicable in areas of more
  45|                         serious defects.
  46|                                                                                               55

===PAGE 56=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                        FLEET MAINTENANCE
   2|                                    The DPW is responsible for the repairs and preventative maintenance to all Town vehicles and equip-
   3|                         ment. High performance police units, first responder Fire and Rescue apparatus, park, cemetery, and school vehi-
   4|                         cles as well as over 30 pieces of highway related machinery are serviced by department personnel. Outsourcing of
   5|                         highly technical repairs has become more frequent and therefore costlier. Due to the volatile global oil market, the
   6|                         overall cost of fuel and petroleum based products continues to be a moving target and therefore difficult to esti-
   7|                         mate annually.
   8|                                    The installation of a vehicle maintenance software program continues to be a valuable tool. Each repair is
   9|                         logged by department and vehicle identification number and memorialized in a data bank. Every vehicle can be
  10|                         tracked for part numbers, required services, as well as frequency and cost of repairs over the life of the equipment.
  11|                         As Director I have created and submitted to Town Officials a vehicle replacement schedule based upon that creat-
  12|                         ed by the American Public Works Association in order to more clearly represent our capital needs.
  13|                         FACILITIES MANAGEMENT
  14|                         The following is a list of buildings and properties maintained by the DPW:
  15|                                          DPW Garage 520 Chase Road                         Public Safety Complex
  16|                                         Park Bldg. Leominster Rd.                           Town Hall
  17|                                          Cemetery Bldg. Holman St.                          Eagle House
  18|                                          Town Beach                                         Town Common
  19|                                          Ritter Bldg.                                       Teen Center
  20|                                          Old Primary School                                 Veterans Park
  21|                                          All athletic fields/playgrounds excluding school properties
  22|                                A facilities staff of two individuals remains on-call 24 hours a day 365 days per year.
  23|                         Staff attempts to keep buildings compliant with current codes to insure a safe secure work environment. Any sig-
  24|                        nificant interruption of service within Town buildings carries the potential of disrupting or disabling necessary
  25|                        services provided to the community. The facilities support staff are problem solvers and technicians with a wide
  26|                         range of experience encompassing all of the construction trades. They deliver services or oversee those functions
  27|                        provided by specialized subcontractors. Staff manages and forecasts all of the building assets and prioritizes re-
  28|                        placement costs. Unfortunately, this component of the capital program has fallen short of expectations. Years of
  29|                         neglect have created a signiticant backlog of necessary repairs and improvements. The process has become reac-
  30|                         tionary at best. The newly formed Building Reuse Committee reported process has become reactionary at best.
  31|                          The Building Reuse Committee reported clearly that asset management relative to our buildings requires a protes-
  32|                         sionally prepared assessment and a 5-year capital plan. Public Works continues its use of a building maintenance
  33|                        software program as a framework for day to day activities. Requests for service are acknowledged immediately,
  34|                        prioritized, and completed in a timely manner. Those individuals requesting service are contacted upon comple-
  35|                        ENVIRONMENTAL
  36|                        Storm Water
  37|                                    As a component of the aforementioned "debt exclusion" approximately five hundred thousand dollars of
  38|                        the four million in total has been earmarked for storm drainage including but not limited to repair of the existing
  39|                         collection system, and additional construction.
  40|                                    As roadways are scheduled for pavement, drainage is surveyed and necessary repairs are addressed to
  41|                        avoid paving over problems. New Federal and State storm water regulations will likely force the Town to become
  42|                        more proactive albeit with no additional identified source of funding.
  43|                                                                                               56

===PAGE 57=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|                                     At the conclusion of winter and again in the late fall drainage structures in close proximity to open wa-
   2|                         ter are cleaned and inspected. Public Works maintains an aggressive schedule of street sweeping and catch basin
   3|                          cleaning, the front line of storm water management.
   4|                          Waste Water
   5|                                    The DPW continues as the caretaker of the entire sewer system. Day to day maintenance of the overall
   6|                         wastewater system inclusive of 10 pump stations is outsourced to a specialty vendor but continues to be the re-
   7|                         sponsibility of Public Works. Business management, inspections, and permitting, remain in-house and the re-
   8|                         sponsibility of the DP W.
   9|                          Tree Removal
  10|                                     Storm damage together with the natural attrition of every species creates a backlog of tree felling and
  11|                         the amount of work far exceeds the available revenue. Until there is a substantial infusion of funds, the DPW will
  12|                         act in a reactionary mode. All work aside from brush removal, chipping, and the felling of small diameter trees is
  13|                         outsourced due to the necessity of a bucket truck or crane and a licensed professional. Each spring DPW staff
  14|                         initiates the activity of removing and disposing of woody debris within the right-of-way together with a Town-
  15|                         wide program of curbside mowing. A aggressive program by our energy provider has lessened the burden of the
  16|                          Town to remove those trees that may cause an interruption of electrical service to the community.
  17|                         Landfill
  18|                                    The "capped" landfill off Youngs Road remains in compliance with the inspection protocol mandated
  19|                         by the Division of Environmental Protection. The site is monitored regularly for excessive methane gas as well
  20|                         as the possibility of groundwater contamination.
  21|                                    The frequency of the testing, reduced through a special permit, acquired as a result of consistently fa-
  22|                         vorable results, remained constant throughout the year. The landfill site including the perimeter fencing is in-
  23|                         spected on a regular schedule by DP W personnel.
  24|                                     Adjacent to the "cap" is a storage area for catch basin materials and street sweepings both determined
  25|                         by the EPA to be hazardous waste. We are permitted to store these materials through the procurement of a
  26|                         (BUD) Beneficial Use Determination permit. The DEP requires yearly testing of these materials. Other commu-
  27|                         nities are required to transport these tainted materials to an appropriate site at an excessive cost.
  28|                                     On weekends during the fall and springtime months DP W personnel oversee and manage the annual
  29|                         composting of brush, leaves and other organic debris providing a valuable service to our residents and add a val-
  30|                         uable component to our recycling program. There has to date been no dedicated funding toward the organization
  31|                         or long range remediation of this location. Once again, dollars will be taken from the General Highway line to
  32|                         maintain this location while keeping it organized and environmentally safe. It is likely this department will pre-
  33|                          sent a plan to use the compost as leverage to clean and grade the area as well as the removal of the brush. With
  34|                          water and sewer at the perimeter this sandy, gravelly area adjacent has been proposed as a potential recreational
  35|                          area for the Town. As of yet this project has not gained traction.
  36|                         Solid Waste
  37|                                     Public works monitors, orders, stores, and distributes trash bags in order to facilitate the curbside collec-
  38|                          tion of trash and recyclables by our current vendor, Casella Inc. Pay as you throw...
  39|                         Lake Shirley Dam
  40|                               In the early 1990s, with the structure in need of repair/rebuilding The Lake Shirley Association turned over
  41|                          ownership of the dam to the Town. After completion of a total reconstruction, responsibilities for maintenance,
  42|                         monitoring, and record keeping was completed solely by a small number of volunteers from the Lake Associa-
  43|                          tion. In 2004-2005 custodianship of the dam, compliance with State dam regulations, the establishment of an
  44|                          operations manual as well as an emergency action plan was placed under the umbrella of the Department of Pub-
  45|                         lic Works. As with the storm water program and other EPA / DEP mandates compliance with current and future
  46|                                                                                               57

===PAGE 58=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                          future regulations is critical. We rely on the expertise and support of a professional consultant to assist with
   2|                          predetermined mandates. The most recent inspection resulted in a positive report.
   3|                          Winter Operations
   4|                                      Snow and ice present a major challenge to our Town. The safety of the general public depends on the
   5|                          ability of the DPW to maintain our roadways in a safe condition despite the severity or the duration of snow and
   6|                          ice events. To that end we have developed a winter operations procedure based upon a detailed plan that must
   7|                          be flexible due to the unpredictability of each storm. A consistently high level of service is expected and must
   8|                          be delivered to every part of Town in a timely manner.
   9|                                      Departmental preparedness begins with successful implementation of the capital plan. Financial sup-
  10|                          port is necessary to provide effective fleet rotation insuring minimal interruption of services due to breakdowns
  11|                          and repairs. In the fall, salt and sand are delivered to the Chase Rd. facility. Lunenburg is in a consortium with
  12|                          14 other communities in an effort to procure the lowest possible price for road salt, an absolute necessity. From
  13|                          late summer to early fall vehicle maintenance staff inspects and prepares the trucks, spreaders and plows for
  14|                          winter operations. During the construction season road crews address right-of-way issues, overhanging branch-
  15|                          es, and pavement defects in order to eliminate obstacles to snow removal. Each division within the DPW is in-
  16|                          volved in winter operations. Typically, DPW staff combines with 18-20 subcontractors to provide service.
  17|                                      Generally, it is not the amount of snow but the duration of the event that determines the overall cost.
  18|                           For example, a fast moving snow storm of 6-8 inches will cost significantly less than a long duration freezing
  19|                          rain event even though the accumulation is much less. Historically, the department operates in a deficit due to a
  20|                          yearly allocation that is below the cost of an average winter.
  21|                                      It is a dedicated group of individuals who successfully challenge the worst Nature has to offer regard-
  22|                          less of severity or duration.
  23|                                                                       "DW workers are the original Storm Troopers"
  24|                          PARKS
  25|                                     In conjunction with the three elected Park Commissioners DPW staff oversees and maintains all parks
  26|                          and grounds exclusive of school property. While resources of the DPW are available as required, much of the
  27|                          Town-wide landscape maintenance is outsourced, resulting in significant savings and increased efficiency over
  28|                          an in-house operation. Repair of the multi-use track complex is imminent. After many years, a mutual effort
  29|                          between the Commissioners and all users of the facility is gaining traction.
  30|                          CEMETERY
  31|                                     One Cemetery Division Superintendent, a DPW employee, handles the duties of the North and South
  32|                          cemeteries, maintaining the grounds, coordinating burials, and meeting regularly with the three members of the
  33|                          Cemetery Commission. The DPW supplies equipment and support staff for, on average, forty burials a year
  34|                          and general maintenance of the facilities.
  35|                                     As always, I am deeply appreciative of the continued patience and support of our residents and fellow
  36|                          Town employees as we persevere.
  37|                                      As caretakers of the Town's infrastructure our goal is to preserve and enhance the resources of the
  38|                          Town of Lunenburg for future generations. To that end we strive to meet or exceed all expectations associated
  39|                          with the challenges of each of the divisions under the umbrella of public works.
  40|                                                                                       Respectfully Submitted
  41|                                                                                           Jack Rodriquenz
  42|                                                                                               Director

   1|                                                               FINANCE COMMITTEE REPORT
   2|                           (This is a reprint of the message printed in the May 6, 2017 Annual Town Meeting Warrant.)
   3|                          The Town Budget to be presented for approval is much the same as it has been in recent
   4|                         years. Our Department Heads were asked to send a 'level service' budget, and requests be-
   5|                         yond level service, to the new Town Manager. Based on the financial forecast and funding
   6|                         opportunities, she is proposing increases beyond level service in some high need areas, such
   7|                         as police and fire, as well as an increase in the schools budget that is funded largely by an
   8|                         increase in Chapter 70 funds from the state. The budget anticipates an increase in revenues
   9|                         from all sources, totaling $1,701,629. Half of this increase is anticipated in state aid through
  10|                         Chapter 70 (education funding) and general government (lottery aid). While this is wel-
  11|                         come news, unrestricted government aid still lags behind earlier levels. Health insurance
  12|                         increases are projected to be less severe than last year.
  13|                         For every $1,000 in revenue to the Town, $643 comes from real estate property taxes. $357
  14|                         comes from other sources such as state aid, motor vehicle or meal taxes, betterments, and
  15|                         other fees. This represents a decrease in reliance on property taxes of about 2.6%. A large
  16|                         portion of this is due to a 13.9% increase in Chapter 70 funds.
  17|                         Another, more tentative source is Free Cash. The effects of state aid on particular aspects of
  18|                         the budget are difficult to parse out, except for Chapter 70 funding, which accounts for $1.21
  19|                         of every $3.00 budgeted for the schools. The School Committee put forward an increase of
  20|                         $1,266,606, which is heavily reliant on the Governor's Chapter 70 spending recommenda-
  21|                         tions making it through the legislature. The School Department has the greatest budget
  22|                         share at 53.4 percent. As reported, property tax funding accounts for about 60 percent of
  23|                         this budget.
  24|                         Because of the positive growth, and lack of new spending requirements, the Town is now in
  25|                         the position to address some ongoing concerns. This budget adds services to Public Safety
  26|                         through 1 Police Officer and 1 Firefighter. Additionally, our Regional Dispatch assessment
  27|                         through the Radio Watch portion of the budget was decreased sufficiently to offset increased
  28|                         weekend coverage at the Public Safety Building. Last year, the Town committed to increas-
  29|                         ing the pavement management plan by $50,000 per year, funded from increases in motor
  30|                         vehicle excise taxes, to ensure that recent improvements paid for through debt exclusion
  31|                         would be adequately maintained going forward. $50,000 of the DP W budget increase ad-
  32|                         dresses this. The Council on Aging is increased $22,080, moving all costs for the Outreach
  33|                         Worker to the Town budget. Historically, part of this position has been covered with grant
  34|                         funds. Most of the rest of the departmental increases address contractual salary obligations.
  35|                                                                                               59

===PAGE 60=== (ocr, 33 lines)
   1|                                                                 FINANCE COMMITTEE REPORT
   2|                            (This is a reprint of the message printed in the May 6, 2017 Annual Town Meeting Warrant.)
   3|                            Last year, we noted a trend of Debt Service, Retirement, and Health Insurance taking up
   4|                           larger portions of the budget, squeezing out other operational needs. This year the trend
   5|                           seems to have hit a pause as increases in Retirement and Insurance has stabilized, and Debt
   6|                            Service has actually decreased by $61,157. The Town for the first time is funding the
   7|                            OPEB Stabilization Fund ($100,000) for future liability for post-employment employee
   8|                           benefits, other than pensions, which has been an issue that the Town has discussed for some
   9|                           time. We continue to weigh the long term effect of adding new positions against the cur-
  10|                           rent needs and opportunities the town faces.
  11|                            The Retirement Assessment has gone up 5 percent this year, compared to 9 percent last
  12|                           year. Town employees pay into 1 of 2 retirement systems. Licensed school district em-
  13|                           ployees contribute to the Massachusetts Teacher Retirement System, which requires no ad-
  14|                           ditional town funding. Any additional funding of this Retirement System is done at the
  15|                           State level, and is reported in notes to the Town Financial Statements as an 'On Behalf'
  16|                           payment. Non -licensed school personnel and Town side employees contribute to the
  17|                           Worcester County Retirement System. The Town makes two payments to this system, an
  18|                           employer contribution for active employees and an assessment to fund the 'Unfunded Lia-
  19|                           bility' of future payments to retirees.
  20|                           The Capital Planning Committee has recommended funding 10 items at $633,317. This
  21|                           budget funds the 10 items. There is no request, nor recommendation to borrow funds to
  22|                           fund any other items. In anticipation of some large capital requests in the coming years (i.e.
  23|                           Fire Trucks), there is a request to form a Capital Stabilization Fund so the Town can set
  24|                           aside funds in a dedicated account to lower the amounts the Town may have to borrow.
  25|                           In summary, the budget presented addresses at least current service levels, with some in-
  26|                           crease in some departments. The Finance Committee will continue to address the question
  27|                           of ensuring the budget addresses the priorities of the Town of Lunenburg. We congratulate
  28|                           the Town administration's work to maintain the AA+ bond rating, and thank all of the town
  29|                            employees for their continued service. We are grateful for the continued cooperation of the
  30|                            Town Manager and each department head to ensure that every question is answered.
  31|                                      Terri Burchfield, Chair; Mark Erickson, Vice Chair; Jay Simeone, Clerk
  32|                                           Hannah Anderson, John Henshaw, George Martin, Mare Luescher
  33|                                                                                               60

===PAGE 61=== (ocr, 20 lines)
   1|                                                                        HISTORICAL COMMISSION
   2|                           The Historical Commission consists of five appointed volunteers who meet once a month
   3|                           from September to June. The work of the Historical Commission is preservation and appreci-
   4|                          ation of all things historic in the Town of Lunenburg. The Historical Commission has through
   5|                          the past year been represented on the Architectural Preservation District Commission
   6|                          (APDC) by two of our members, Richard McGrath and Cullen Dwyer. They have worked
   7|                           with the other members of the APDC to finish the Design Standards of the APDC Bylaw.
   8|                           We are nearing completion of the Bronze Marker Project, which was made possible through
   9|                          the efforts of Eagle Scout Joshua Herrick of Lunenburg Troop 1728. All the houses in Lunen-
  10|                           burg's National Register Historic District were surveyed for plaques (last available in 1987)
  11|                          and those homes missing plaques were purchased by the Historic Commission from the Erie
  12|                          Landmark Company. We are awaiting delivery. Installation will happen this spring and all the
  13|                          houses in our National Registered Historic District will finally get the recognition they de-
  14|                           serve.
  15|                           We continue to hold hearings and enact the Demolition Delay Bylaw of the Town for the de-
  16|                           struction of Historic properties. This past year we saw the loss of an 1800's barn on West
  17|                           Townsend Road.
  18|                          Historical Commission Members:
  19|                           Rebecca Lantry, Richard McGrath, Cullen Dwyer, Jennifer Sanderson and Bill Lakso.
