===PAGE 62=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|                                                                   INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY
   2|                        The primary mission of the Information Technology Department is to increase efficiency through
   3|                        the use of technology. The IT Department enables all other town departments and schools to
   4|                        communicate and collaborate, manage daily tasks, organize, store and share information, and
   5|                        generally conduct Town business more effectively. We also provide the necessary infrastructure,
   6|                        equipment, software and expertise to comply with current regulations and methodology when
   7|                        interacting with outside agencies and organizations.
   8|                        The Information Technology staff for the Town has remained constant this year, but we have
   9|                        added a new full time IT position for the Schools. Following is an overview of our current IT
  10|                        Steve Malandrinos - Information Technology Director
  11|                              • Hired in December, 2012
  12|                                    Previously an engineer at Cisco Systems, IT Director for the Town of Belchertown, MA
  13|                                    and Belchertown Public Schools
  14|                        Daniel Nadareski - Network Administrator
  15|                              • Retired police officer from the Albany, NY police force with over 24 years on the job
  16|                              • Prior experience includes computer crime and forensics, network administration, video
  17|                                    security system administration, and IP phone system administration
  18|                        Jordan Pochini - Tech Support Specialist
  19|                              • Recently graduated from Fitchburg State University with a degree in Computer Science
  20|                              • Prior experience includes both individual contributor and management roles in retail
  21|                                    environments
  22|                        Nicholas Scoggins - School Technology Coordinator
  23|                              • Received Associate and Bachelor of Science degrees from ITT Technical Institute
  24|                              • Served 5 years as Security System Administrator at Greater Lawrence Technical School
  25|                        Along with serving as helpdesk for all users' daily technology issues and performing regular
  26|                        maintenance and scheduled replacement of the town's and schools' technology assets, the IT
  27|                        Department has been involved with several high impact projects throughout the past year.
  28|                        Over the past few months, we have worked with an information security firm to perform a cyber-
  29|                        security audit of our user base. The audit has consisted of several mock phishing campaigns,
  30|                        designed to test our users' awareness of and reaction to attempts to illicitly gain access to Town
  31|                        computer systems. The goal of the audit is to raise awareness of one of the most prevalent
  32|                        malware vectors used to infiltrate computer networks, and with each iteration and subsequent
  33|                        training, our failure rate has improved significantly.
  34|                        We have also worked closely with the Town Manager on a complete upgrade and redesign of the
  35|                        Town website. With citizen engagement top of mind, we are adding new ways to keep abreast of
  36|                        what is happening, as well as have your voice heard and get involved. Our new homepage will
  37|                        prominently feature our official Facebook feed, and options to sign up for alerts when things
  38|                        change or new information is added. Our goal is to keep the information flowing - in both
  39|                        directions - so that everyone has a chance to effectively participate in the process.
  40|                                                                                               62

===PAGE 63=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                                                                      LUNENBURG PUBLIC LIBRARY
   2|                       The Library had a very busy year (67,762 people came through our doors) but it has also been very fun!
   3|                       When I look back on the last year it seems centered on three actions: COLLABORATE,
   4|                       COMMUNICATE and CREATE. We have stepped things up in each of these three areas. Collaborating
   5|                       with individuals and local organizations has been a primary focus of the Library this year - even if to
   6|                       make it happen we had to tweak a few of our more traditional rules!. The Library partnered with Project
   7|                       Bread and its Meals4Kids program to bring nutritious lunches to children throughout the summer months.
   8|                        We have also begun to use our communication vehicles like our Facebook page and our website as well
   9|                       as leveraging the skills of some of the Library's staff to help other organizations get the word out on a
  10|                       special event or activity and/or improve the look of their communications. The Library provided space
  11|                       and assistance for the activities of local organizations like GiftMart, the Women's Club Christmas Basket
  12|                       effort, Coloring for Cancer campaign, the Scouts and many more. In recognition of the fact that more and
  13|                       more people go to school online the Library has been proctoring exams for some students (high school
  14|                       and college level). The Library has created new traditions with our introduction of an Artisan's Fair and
  15|                       Family Craft Night (cocoa & crafts after the Town Tree Lighting). The Library introduced a monthly e-
  16|                        newsletter so we can push the latest news and events out to our users.
  17|                       During the past year with the help of the Friends of the Library organization and several volunteers we
  18|                       provided two Book & Bake Sales, began reupholstering some "well-loved" furniture, and moved the
  19|                       entire (14,000+ books) adult collection to improve accessibility and make room for growth. The Library
  20|                       also introduced two new program series on Fundraising & Grants and Our Democracy. Funding provided
  21|                       by the Friends organization and the Cultural Council helped to provide 650 programs. The Library also
  22|                       hosted over 300 meetings and events for other local organizations.
  23|                       The Children's Room continues to be a popular destination for children and families throughout the
  24|                        region. Collection evaluation and development continues as we strive to provide the most up-to-date,
  25|                       substantive materials for our young patrons. The beginning chapter book area that we created for
  26|                        emergent readers has outgrown its initial location and now has a new home with lots more room for
  27|                       display and expansion. The non-fiction area for preschool aged children is heavily used and we are
  28|                        continually adding new items. Nonfiction books give children a chance to learn new concepts and
  29|                       vocabulary, as well as broaden their view of the world. Educators and publishers are recognizing that this
  30|                       interest begins at a very early age for many children; therefore, the book options are wonderful!
  31|                        We are constantly evaluating our toy/manipulative collection to keep it current, stimulating and in good
  32|                       condition. Toys are more than just fun and games for kids. Playing with toys engages a child's senses,
  33|                       sparks their imaginations and encourages them to interact with others. We are fortunate to have had many
  34|                       quality items donated for our use.
  35|                        We also have enrichment activities designed for school aged children. Lego Club which is offered with a
  36|                       monthly Lego Challenge is well attended by both girls and boys and encourages child/parent interaction.
  37|                       Our Tuesdays with Stories Book Club also occurs monthly and the children are enjoying the lively book
  38|                       discussions and related snacks and crafts which are all part of this program. Monthly S.T.E.M.
  39|                       programming is also provided for elementary aged children. Our goal with these fun science, technology,
  40|                       engineering and math activities is to encourage and excite these young learners. A strong knowledge
  41|                       base in these core subjects - science, technology, engineering, and math (STEM) is one of the keys to a
  42|                        successful future.
  43|                                                                                               63

===PAGE 64=== (ocr, 25 lines)
   1|                        Usage of the Story Craft Room by Lunenburg Boy and Girl Scout Troops has increased. These
   2|                        organizations have discovered that we provide a friendly, warm, clean meeting space with lots of parking.
   3|                        The added benefit to these children and their families is access to our wonderful library materials.
   4|                        The Teen Room saw increased attendance, new partnerships and more in 2017. The amount of hours
   5|                       read for the 2017 Summer Reading Program doubled to a whopping 447 hours read in response to a
   6|                        change in the prize structure. Program participation ran strong with 175 tweens and teens attending 14
   7|                        programs.
   8|                        A partnership with the Boy's and Girl's Club resulted in a new program, Homework Power Hour, where
   9|                        students in grades 6-12 can come to the library after school to do their homework. This provides students
  10|                        with a quiet space to study as well as access to computers. In addition, National Honor Society students
  11|                        have the opportunity to earn service hours by tutoring. This partnership also allowed for offering a Mad
  12|                       Science Escape Room as part of the Shrieks and Krieks Haunted House, bringing a drop in library
  13|                       program to those who are unable to attend the afterschool surprise sessions.
  14|                       In response for the increased demand for fiction, graphic novels, audio books, and manga, the young adult
  15|                        nonfiction collection was interfiled with the adult collection. This resulted in more shelf space for the
  16|                        high demand collections while also filing all nonfiction resources together to make researching easier.
  17|                        On a final note, teen led programming was introduced in December with the plastic canvas holiday craft
  18|                        program. While the library provided space and supplies, this workshop was created and led by a teen who
  19|                        wanted to share the needlepointing skill she had learned over the summer. The hope is to introduce more
  20|                        of this type of programming in the future as it encourages public speaking, leadership skills, and fosters a
  21|                        sense of ownership in library.

  23|                        Martha Moore, Director

   1|                           Studio and Equipment Upgrades
   2|                           PAC has had studio space in the TC Passios school since 2013. We encourage the public to
   3|                           take advantage of the facilities and equipment offered.
   4|                           The strategy for the PAC studio in the upcoming Fiscal years includes space planning for a
   5|                           new studio and to continue improvements in the current studio. Part of the plan is to up-
   6|                           grade the current audio and lighting systems to increase the quality of the content produced. We would
   7|                           like to increase visibility in the community by mainly encouraging more studio usage, as well as loaning out
   8|                           equipment to the public.
   9|                           In FY17 PAC acquired four new JVC cameras and a wireless microphone system for Town
  10|                           Hall. The wireless mics allow boards and committees the ability to conduct workshop
  11|                           meetings in the Bilotta Room at the center conference table. Providing this audio flexibility
  12|                           has enhanced board members participation with the public. The new cameras are current-
  13|                           ly being used by the PAC staff.
  14|                           Remote live broadcasting was improved with the addition of a DataVideo converter, which
  15|                           transfers a live signal through the internet to the CASTUS server and out to the public on
  16|                           Ch 8 or Ch 9. Live feeds are now possible from the PAC studio, the LMHS Auditorium, and
  17|                           the Public Safety Building.
  18|                           Equipment purchases accounted for 25% of the FY17 expenses.
  19|                           Staff, Producers and Volunteers
  20|                           In FY17, there was one full-time Program Coordinator town employee and four part-time
  21|                           staff, classified as Vendors (Budget Line Item: "Purchase of Services").
  22|                           The Program Coordinator managed the day-to-day operation of the organization, including
  23|                           all production and programming schedules, equipment and services procurement, studio
  24|                           and inventory administration, advertising, and social media. Vendors provided video re-
  25|                           cording of committee meetings and additional video assignments, as needed.

   1|                                                     SENIOR PROPERTY TAX WORK-OFF PROGRAM
   2|                                                                                          Est. 1998
   3|                                                              CALENDAR YEAR JAN. 1, 2017-NOV. 1, 2017
   4|                            The Property Tax Work-Off Committee is a five member committee appointed by the Board of Selectmen. The
   5|                            Lunenburg Property Tax Work-Off Program offers income eligible senior citizens age 60+ an opportunity to
   6|                           receive up to $1,000 off their property tax bill in the calendar year rather than the fiscal year. Participants
   7|                            worked in various town departments to earn the tax abatement doing general office duties, custodial, library
   8|                            shelving, and kitchen assistance. For each hour worked a person received $10 and worked up to 100 hours be-
   9|                            tween January 1, 2017 and November 1, 2017 to receive the abatement. Any hours worked beyond that were
  10|                            considered volunteer hours.
  11|                            There were nine Lunenburg residents who participated between January 1, 2017 to November 1, 2017. These
  12|                            individuals, as a whole, accumulated 862 hours towards their abatements.
  13|                           Eligibility Requirements:
  14|                                 Applicants must be 60 years of age or older
  15|                                 Own and occupy a home in Lunenburg as their primary residence for at least one year
  16|                                 Receive a property tax bill in their name or their spouse's name who is also 60 years of age or older
  17|                                 Meet the income guidelines of :
  18|                                       One person household - $40,000 / Two person household - $60,000
  19|                                 All new applicants are required to have a CORI background check and must re-apply and be accepted
  20|                                       annually for participation in the program.
  21|                           Earnings are exempt from state taxes only. Earnings are subject to federal and FICA tax withholdings and are
  22|                           also subject to OBRA if participants are not receiving a pension from either Worcester Regional Retirement or
  23|                            Massachusetts State Teachers Retirement.
  24|                            Departments who participated between January 1, 2017 - November 1, 2017:
  25|                            Board of Selectmen
  26|                           Board of Health
  27|                            Building Department
  28|                            Council on Aging
  29|                           DPW/Sewer Department
  30|                           Public Library
  31|                           School Department
  32|                            Treasurer
  33|                                                                                               70

===PAGE 71=== (ocr, 49 lines)
   1|                                                                              SEWER COMMISSION
   2|                       The five member Sewer Commission was established at the May 6, 2006 Special Town Meeting. At the first
   3|                       the new fiscal year, Carl Luck was elected to remain as Chairman, John Reynolds as Vice-Chair and Sue Bunish
   4|                       as Clerk. Both Carl Luck and John Reynolds were elected to 3 year terms at the May 2016 election. There
   5|                       remained two open positions on the Commission after a failure to elect at the May election and Joseph Anderson
   6|                       stepped forward to fill one vacancy after appointment by the Selectmen and Commission on July 5, 2016. On
   7|                       July 19, 2016, Joan Pease was appointed by the Selectmen and Commission to fill the other vacancy. When Sue
   8|                       Bunish submitted her letter of resignation in July 2016, Richard St. Cyr was appointed to fill that vacancy on
   9|                       October 11, 2016. He remained until February 26, 2017, when he resigned to move out of town. With Sue's
  10|                       departure, Joe Anderson was elected to serve as Clerk for the Commission on July 26, 2016. At the May 2017
  11|                       election in, Troy Daniels was elected to the one-year term opened by the departure of Richard St. Cyr by write-
  12|                       in vote.
  13|                       The Commission bears the responsibility of overseeing the Sewer Enterprise Fund, planning for the future and
  14|                       developing and implementing rules, regulations and policies. The Department of Public Works manages and
  15|                       maintains the operational side of the system. Under the Town Charter, responsibility for the day-to day
  16|                       operation and care and maintenance of the sanitary sewers are under the supervision of the Town Manager.
  17|                       Lunenburg does not operate a sewer treatment plant. Approximately 83% of Lunenburg sewer is discharged to
  18|                       Leominster and the other 17% is discharged to Fitchburg. There are currently 862 properties connected to the
  19|                       Lunenburg system and another 381 that have access to Town sewer but are not yet connected. During FY 17, 44
  20|                       new accounts connected to town sewer. The Commission billed out $763,329 in quarterly sewer bills and had
  21|                       $63,087 of prior year delinquencies added to the owners' property tax bills for non-payment. Eighty-six
  22|                       properties gained access to sewer with the completion of the SRF project in the Pratt St., Pleasantview Ave. and
  23|                       Lakeview Ave. areas of town and were assessed a betterment of $13,997.47 to pay down the debt owed by the
  24|                       Town for the SRF project. One additional property was assessed a privilege fee in FY17 for increased usage as
  25|                       a multi-family residence. Privilege fees help to pay down the debt owed by the Town on the original sewer
  26|                       project.
  27|                       The Commission continues contracting with Small Water Systems Services of Littleton (SWSS) for the
  28|                       Operations and Management of the ten pumping stations and sewer infrastructure. The Commission has been
  29|                       very satisfied with the personnel, the work and the arrangement with SWSS, renewing the contract for a second
  30|                       three-year renewable contract when SWSS was awarded the bid in July 2016. The new contract, effective
  31|                       7/1/16, was expanded to include the generators in each pump station and quarterly meter readings for manual
  32|                       and irrigation accounts.
  33|                       In July 2016, with the new Grease Trap regulation in place, the Commission commenced the annual permit
  34|                       program with the new fee structure and requirements for annual permits, compliance and reporting. Lunenburg
  35|                       has eighteen food service establishments with grease traps and interceptors. This regulation continues to be
  36|                       strongly enforced as Fats, Oils and Grease (FOG) can negatively affect the smooth operation and maintenance
  37|                       costs of the sewer system. While the Grease Trap Regulation focuses on commercial and public disposal,
  38|                       individual home owners can also contribute a significant amount of grease into the system which increases
  39|                       everyone's costs. Whether on sewer or septic, grease should not be put down the drain.
  40|                        At the May 2017 Town Meeting, the Commission put forward four articles, three of which related to the budget
  41|                       and stabilization funds. For the standard FY18 budget article, $560,169.06 was appropriated from Sewer
  42|                       Enterprise revenues, $10,285 was transferred from the Sewer Reserve Capacity Stabilization Fund and $14,520
  43|                       from the Sewer Inflow/Infiltration Stabilization Fund and appropriated from Sewer Enterprise retained earnings
  44|                       the sum of $160,276.94 for a total appropriation of $745,251 to operate the Sewer Enterprise Fund for FY18.
  45|                       $911,971.24 was included in appropriations from the General Fund to cover both general expenses and debt
  46|                       service on the betterments. The goal of the Commission is to maintain a balanced budget and build up the
  47|                       Retained Earnings so that it may be used for major capital projects to maintain and improve the operating
  48|                       efficiency of the system. The second article transferred monies to the Reserve Capacity Stabilization Fund and
  49|                                                                                               71

===PAGE 72=== (ocr, 23 lines)
   1|                          the third article was to create a Stabilization fund for Capital Reserve. 3% of proposed revenues were transferred
   2|                          to Capital Reserve Stabilization Fund. The fourth article was to accept c. 40, § 58 of the General Laws to
   3|                          impose a "municipal charges lien" on real property for unpaid sewer connection fees, Sewer Reserve Capacity
   4|                          fees, Sewer Re-connection fees, and Sewer Grease Trap permit fees owed the Town in the manner as provided
   5|                         for by MGL c. 40, 8 58.
   6|                          Engineering consulting firms, Wright Pierce and Weston & Sampson have continued to assist the Commission
   7|                         in working to identify and reduce Inflow and Infiltration (I/I) and the effort continues to be the focus of the
   8|                          Commission going into FY18.
   9|                          The Commission would like to thank Sue Bunish and Richard St. Cyr for their service and welcomes Joe
  10|                          Anderson, Joan Pease and the return of Troy Daniels in FY17. Bob Ebersole continues to serve as the Board of'
  11|                          Selectmen liaison and the Commission is very grateful for his knowledge and counsel. The Commission would
  12|                          also like to thank DPW Director Jack Rodriquenz for the seamless and smooth operation of the Sewer Division
  13|                         of the DPW and Jim Breault (DPW-Facilities) who whenever called upon, provides his expertise and service to
  14|                         the Sewer Division. They would also like to acknowledge and thank the Lunenburg Water District for their
  15|                         partnership and good working relationship with the DPW and especially with Business Manager, Barb Lefebvre,
  16|                          in providing valuable assistance with meter readings, ownership changes, and status updates.
  17|                          Sewer Commission as of February 2018:
  18|                          Joe Anderson, Chair
  19|                          Carl Luck, Vice Chair
  20|                          Joan Pease, Clerk
  21|                         John Reynolds, Member
  22|                         Troy Daniels, Member

   1|                                                                            BOARD OF HEALTH
   2|                       The Board of Health Office is located in the Ritter Memorial Building on the corner of Mass. Ave
   3|                       and Leominster Road. Our office is on the upper level at the end of the hall on the right. We can
   4|                       be reached at 978-345-4146, extension 430.
   5|                       The Board of Health, with the assistance of its Agent, the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health,
   6|                       continued its mission to maintain its focus on public health and safety issues during 2017,
   7|                       reviewing Title V Inspection Reports, building permits, septic and well permits, nuisance
   8|                       investigations, housing inspections, complaints and general public health related issues.
   9|                       The Board of Health oversees the Septic System Repair Program which helps low to moderate
  10|                       income families replace failed septic systems or connect to public sewer. Anyone interested in
  11|                       finding out more about this program can contact the office.
  12|                       Homeland Security planning is ongoing. Meetings continue between our agents, other Town
  13|                       departments and regional entities to develop a plan for not only Lunenburg but with our partner
  14|                       towns in our region.
  15|                       This year we processed applications for septic permit lot tests, new construction permits, new
  16|                       wells, repairs or upgrades to septic systems and well permits. The Board continues its vigilance
  17|                       with regard to septic systems, wells, food service inspections, housing and nuisance complaints
  18|                       and its general mandate to protect the environment and groundwater of the Commonwealth.
  19|                       The Board of Health provides numerous other services in cooperation with our Health Agents, the
  20|                       Nashoba Associated Boards of Health. In addition to our environmental services, numerous
  21|                       clinics are provided throughout the year to our residents. These clinics provide inoculations,
  22|                       blood pressure screenings, Well Oldster Clinics and dental screening. Fluoride treatment and
  23|                       dental checks of our youngsters in the school system are provided by the Nashoba dental staff.
  24|                       Lunenburg participates in the Central Mass. Mosquito Control Project. Mosquito Control
  25|                       monitors the mosquito population in Lunenburg and advises of any potential diseases such as
  26|                       EEE and West Nile Virus and sprays frequently to control the population during the warmer
  27|                       times of the year. Residents who want to have their property sprayed can do so by emailing to:
  28|                       cтmcp@cmmcp.org, calling (508) 393-3055, completing a request on their webpage at:
  29|                       http://www.cmmcp.org, or calling the Board of Health office.
  30|                       With our Nashoba Nursing and Hospice partners we provide in home visits to residents
  31|                       recovering from illness and hospice care and counseling to those in need of this service.
  32|                       Please contact our office in the Ritter Memorial Building for more information.
  33|                       Please remember that our Board Members volunteer their time and efforts on behalf of you, the
  34|                       citizens of Lunenburg. If you have something that you think we can help you with, we are at your
  35|                       service. Contact the Board office at 582-4146 ext. 430.
  36|                                                                                               74

   3|                                                  NASHOBA ASSOCIATED BOARDS OF HEALTH
   4|                                    Nashoba Associated Boards of Health continues to serve the local Board of Health in
   5|                       meeting the challenges of environmental and public health needs in Lunenburg. In addition to the
   6|                       day to day public health work conducted for Lunenburg we also provide the following services.
   7|                             • Maintaining Nashoba's internet web site to provide information for the public. (See
   8|                                    http://www.nashoba.org/
   9|                             • Response to health related impacts of beavers through coordination with the local Board of
  10|                                   Health in the issuance of the necessary permits.
  11|                             • Response to state mandated changes in regulations requiring more frequent beach
  12|                                   sampling through sample collection, submittal for analysis, and follow-up when results
  13|                                    were obtained.
  14|                                    We look forward to continuing our work with Lunenburg's Board of Health. Included in
  15|                       the day-to-day work of Nashoba in 2017 were the tollowing:
  16|                                    Through membership in the Association Lunenburg benefited from the services of
  17|                                    Nashoba staff including: Registered Sanitarians, Certified Health Officers, Registered
  18|                                    Nurses, Nutritionists, Registered Physical & Occupational Therapists, Licensed Social
  19|                                    Workers, Certified Home Health Aides, and Registered Dental Hygienists
  20|                             • Provided health education programs in collaboration with the Lunenburg Council on
  21|                                   Aging.
  22|                             • Collaborated with Montachusett Home Care around elders at risk and other safety
  23|                                    issues.
  24|                             • Reviewed 108 Title 5 state mandated private Septic System Inspections for Lunenburg
  25|                                    Board of Health. Received, reviewed, and filed these state mandated (but unfunded) Title
  26|                                    5 inspections. Corresponded with inspectors regarding deficiencies; referred deficient
  27|                                   inspections to Lunenburg Board of Health for enforcement action

  36|                                                             Nashoba secretaries during daily business hours.
  37|                                                Food Service Licenses & Inspections.....
  38|                                                             Nashoba annually mails out and receives application from both
  39|                                                restaurants and retail food businesses. Licenses are renewed for existing
  40|                                                facilities. Plans are submitted and reviewed prior to initial licensing. Most
  41|                                                licensees are inspected at a minimum twice a year. Where deficiencies are
  42|                                                found, a re-inspection is scheduled to insure compliance. When a complaint
  43|                                                from the public is received an inspection is also conducted. During this
  44|                                                inspection health factors of food handlers is also investigated, and where
  45|                                                appropriate medical consultation and laboratory testing may be required.

===PAGE 76=== (ocr, 49 lines)
   1|                                    Beach/Camp Inspections.............                                            ...... 13
   2|                                                             Camps are inspected at least annually at opening for compliance with
   3|                                                State Sanitary Code, Chapter IV, 105 CMR 430.00. Public swimming beaches
   4|                                                are sampled for water quality every week during the summer and more often if a
   5|                                                problem is suspected.
   6|                                                Housing & Nuisance Investigations.
   7|                                                             Nashoba, as agent for the local Board of Health, inspects dwellings for
   8|                                                conformance with State Sanitary Code, 105 CMR 410.00, issues orders for
   9|                                                 correction, and re-inspect for compliance. Similarly, complaints from residents
  10|                                                concerning unsanitary conditions or pollution events are investigated.
  11|                                                Septic System Test Applications.                                                   ...42
  12|                                                             Applications from residents proposing to build or upgrade a septic system
  13|                                                are accepted, a file created, and testing dates are coordinated with the applicants
  14|                                                engineer.
  15|                                                Septic System Lot Tests....                                           ....120
  16|                                                             Nashoba sanitarian witnesses soil evaluations, deep hole tests, and,
  17|                                                             usually on a separate date, percolation tests conducted by the applicant's
  18|                                                             engineer which serve as the basis of the design of the septic system.
  19|                                                Septic System Plan Applications...                                                    ..47
  20|                                                             Detailed plans created by engineers hired by residents proposing to build
  21|                                                or upgrade a septic system are received, filed, and routed to the Nashoba
  22|                                                 sanitarian for review.
  23|                                                Septic System Plan Reviews ....                                         ...81
  24|                                                                          Engineered plans are reviewed according to state code, Title 5,
  25|                                                             and local Board of Health regulations and review forms are created and
  26|                                                             sent to engineer for revision. Subsequent re-submittals by the engineer
  27|                                                             are also reviewed.
  28|                                                Septic System Permit Applications (new lots)....                                 ...17
  29|                                                 Septic System Permit Applications (upgrades) .......•...• 36

  31|                                                Septic System Construction Inspections...
  32|                                               Nashoba Sanitarian is called to construction site at various phases of
  33|                                               construction to witness & verify that system is built according to plans.
  34|                                                Septic System Consultations....                               ............
  35|                                                During all phases of application, design, and construction the Nashoba

  18|                                    Community Health Nursing
  19|                                                Nashoba's Community Health Nursing program provides an essential public hea
  20|                                                service to it member communities guided by the American Nurses Associat
  21|                                                (ANA) 16 Standards of Public Nursing Practice.
  22|                                                Our staff serves as a resource to the community, addressing questions and conce
  23|                                                from the public on a variety of health and safety issues (immunizations, head li
  24|                                                chronic disease management, safe drug/sharps disposal etc.) in a variety of ways.
  25|                             • We conduct regular well-being clinics for health assessment, screenings and education
  26|                                   to all, especially the underserved and at-risk populations.
  27|                             • We report and investigate communicable diseases to formulate and implement control
  28|                                    guidelines and strategies that protect the health of individual and the community at
  29|                                   large (mandated by the Massachusetts Department of Public Health).
  30|                             • We make will-being visits to assess needs, coordinate appropriate care and services and
  31|                                   provide case management as needed (health promotion).
  32|                             • We provide public health education.
  33|                              • We address psych-social issues that may impact general health and safety (i.e.
  34|                                   hoarding).
  35|                       We are available to collaborate with all municipal staff to address public health nursing
  36|                       questions, work closely with Councils on Aging to assist elders in the communities and are
  37|                       available for consultation with school nurses caring for children and families. The nursing staff
  38|                       also works with the Sanitarian for your community, as needed, to address issues of unhealthy
  39|                       living conditions.
  40|                                    Listed below is summary of the activities of the Community Health Nursing
  41|                       program.
  42|                       Nashoba conducted 11 clinics and those clinics offered your citizens: blood pressure screening,
  43|                       annual sugar and eye screenings, flu shots and an opportunity to consult with the nurse to
  44|                       address questions.
  45|                       Our staff conducted 8 health promotion/well-being/social worker visits in your communities.

   1|                                                   TOWN OF LUNENBURG, MASSACHUSETTS
   2|                                                                     FIRE DEPARTMENT
   3|                             CHIEF                                                                                                              655 Massachusetts Ave
   4|                          Patrick A. Sullivan                                                                                                   Lunenburg, MA 01462
   5|                          TEL: (978) 582-4155
   6|                          FAX: (978) 582-4033
   7|                                                                                             118
   8|                               The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are dedicated to providing the
   9|                                 highest quality, most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to the citizens of the town of Lunenburg.
  10|                                       In 2017 your Fire Department responded to 2107 calls for service, broken down as follows:
  11|                                       Fires & Hazardous Conditions                 514               Emergency Medical 1022
  12|                                       Inspections                                  587               Other                 20
  13|                                       Fire Department staffing as of July 1, 2017 is 5 Career and 40-45 On Call Firefighters and Emergency Medical
  14|                              Technicians, plus 1 full time Administrative Assistant/EMS Coordinator. The Station is manned 7 days per week from 6am-6pm
  15|                              with 2-3 Firefighter/Emergency Medical Technicians, plus the Chief of Department and EMS Coordinator weekdays. The Station
  16|                              is also staffed two nights per week with 2 Firefighter/EMT's. This coverage is a combination of Full Time personnel in
  17|                              conjunction with 84 hours per week of per diem coverage. The per diem coverage is provided by our Call Firefighter/EMT's
  18|                              manning the Station at their hourly Call rate.
  19|                                       Sunday through Thursday nights however, the Station is unmanned and all of our personnel respond from home on an
  20|                              "on call" basis. This adds several minutes or more to our response time as personnel must respond to the station, pick up the
  21|                              appropriate equipment and then respond the call.
  22|                                       The call volume in 2017 was an overall increase of 3% over 2016. We also experienced a 6% increase in Emergency
  23|                              Medical related calls, which is consistent with the growth we have seen over the last 10 years. As our community continues to
  24|                              grow, the demand for emergency services grows along with it. I must continue to state the need for 24 hour staffing in the
  25|                              Station. Funding to maintain a minimum of 2 Firefighter/EMT's or Paramedics in the Station 24 hours a day, 7 days a week is
  26|                              critical to maintain the high level of service our Townspeople expect. This would allow either the first ambulance or first piece of
  27|                              fire apparatus to respond immediately. Not only would this reduce our response times to critical emergencies, it would also
  28|                              reduce the "wear and tear" on our Call members. This coverage would in no way reduce the need for our On Call personnel; it
  29|                              would only supplement their response and reduce response times.
  30|                                       Staffing was slightly increased on July 1, 2017 with the addition of 1 Full Time member. Lt. Christos Lekaditis joined
  31|                              the ranks of our full time staff and functions as our Assistant Fire Prevention and Training Officer. Christos was a Call Firefighter
  32|                              with the Department for 10 years and also served as a Full Time Firefighter with the Devens Fire Department. The addition of
  33|                              this full time position allowed us to slightly increase our coverage during the day time, and to begin covering two nights per week
  34|                              as well. It should be noted that an additional 5 positions are needed to provide 2 personnel in the Station 24/7, plus an additional
  35|                              member weekdays when most of our Call Personnel are out of town.
  36|                                       As we plan towards additional staffing in the future, consideration for transitioning the Ambulance Service to the
  37|                              Paramedic Level of care must also be considered. We currently operate two Ambulances at the Advanced EMT Level, but must
  38|                              rely on outside agencies for assistance with full Advanced Life Support Care. Hiring Paramedic Level trained Firefighters and
  39|                              equipping the ambulances to that level will bring a higher level of care to our residents in a faster, more efficient way.
  40|                              Recruitment and Retention of our Call Firefighters continues to be one of the greatest challenges for the Department, and is my
  41|                              main reason to call for more permanent staffing in the Station. Make no mistake, our Call personnel do an outstanding job, and
  42|                              their commitment never ceases to amaze me. However, like many Call Departments we suffer from a continual turnover
  43|                                                                                               79

===PAGE 80=== (ocr, 53 lines)
   1|                                   in personnel. Public Safety is a field where training and experience are critical. A new Call Firefighter/EMT must
   2|                        attend over 340 hours of initial training, plus additional time in orientation and field training. Members qualifying to drive Fire
   3|                        Apparatus must complete an additional 65 hours of classroom and practical training. On average, it takes a new member one
   4|                        and a half to two years to properly complete these requirements. Unfortunately, for a variety of reasons we often do not retain
   5|                        members for more than 5-6 years.
   6|                                   As an example, during 2016-2017 6 of our most active Call Firefighters obtained Full time Firefighting Positions with
   7|                        other Departments, and another member joined the Armed Services. While most have remained members of the Department,
   8|                       their availability to be "On Call" and cover per diem shifts has been significantly reduced.
   9|                                   In 2017, the Fire Prevention Division conducted 587 Inspections. This is in addition to multiple fire drills and public
  10|                        education initiatives. By law we are required to inspect Smoke and Carbon Monoxide Detector installations when a home is sold,
  11|                       as well as all new oil burner and propane gas installations. Any business holding a liquor license is also required to be inspected
  12|                       for fire safety issues as well. In addition, all new construction projects must have their plans reviewed for proper fire protection,
  13|                        as well as ongoing inspections during the construction process. Personnel also continue to provide fire safety education to not
  14|                        only children in our schools but adults as well.    Other Fire Safety Initiatives undertaken during 2017 include:
  15|                                        Continuation of a Department of Fire Services Grant in the amount of $6953 to assist with Fire Safety Education in
  16|                                        both the Schools and with Senior Citizens. This Grant provides funding for both materials and personnel to bring
  17|                                        fire safety education to our most vulnerable populations. Highlights of activity through this grant include:
  18|                                              • Educational programs in basic fire safety in Grades K-3, including use of the Regional Fire Safety
  19|                                                    Trailer.
  20|                                              • Educational programs in conjunction with the Council on Aging. These included cooking and heating
  21|                                                    safety, a CPR Demonstration and Ice safety.
  22|                                   - Participation in the Senior Center's "Sand for Seniors" Program. This program provides buckets of walkway sand
  23|                                         for Senior Citizens who were unable to obtain it for themselves in the winter months. In partnership with this
  24|                                         program, Captain James Ricci delivers the sand to the residents and when possible, inspects their homes for
  25|                                         Smoke and Carbon Monoxide Detectors. If the resident was lacking detectors, Capt. Ricci is able to install free
  26|                                         detectors obtained through the Senior SAFE Grant.
  27|                                      It should be noted that each year, 50% of fatal fires are found to have nonfunctional or nonexistent smoke
  28|                                         detectors.
  29|                                   Firefighters Joseph Arsenault and Stephen Morse graduated from the Massachusetts Firefighting Academy's
  30|                        Call/Volunteer Training Program. This intensive 4 month program provides the basic skills firefighters need to operate in today's
  31|                        challenging environments. All newly hired Firefighters are required to complete this program within one year of hire.
  32|                                   10 Firefighters from Lunenburg have undergone specialized training and respond as part of the District Technical
  33|                        Rescue and Dive Teams. These regional teams provide specially trained personnel and equipment beyond what most
  34|                        communities are able to provide at the local level. In conjunction with these Teams, Lunenburg operates both a Water Rescue
  35|                        Unit and a Collapse/Trench Rescue Trailer. In September, Chief Sullivan, Capt. Ken Jones and Firefighter Ben Boudreau
  36|                        participated in Operation SCORE, a Disaster Drill designed to test the capabilities of the State's Regional Technical Rescue
  37|                       Teams. Our members, along with 50+ other Firefighters from Worcester County traveled to Cape Cod and operated through the
  38|                       night at a simulated community destroyed by a severe weather event. Crews stabilized damaged structures and conducted
  39|                       rescues of simulated victims. This was the first full scale deployment and test of the Worcester County Teams. Our Region's
  40|                        Team was one of the few that was able to fully complete all tasks assigned to them, and completed them ahead of schedule.
  41|                                   Apparatus replacement continues to be a concern. Two of our front line fire apparatus (Engine 4 and Ladder 1) are 22
  42|                        and 29 years old years old respectively. Both units are showing their age through increasing maintenance costs and "out of
  43|                        service" time. These units have been submitted for replacement on the FY'19 Capital Plan (both having been turned down in
  44|                        FY'18) and it is hoped that due consideration will be given for their timely replacement. Rescue 2, a 2008 Ambulance has also
  45|                        been submitted for replacement as it is near the end of its projected life span. The high reliability required of front line
  46|                        emergency apparatus becomes increasingly difficult to maintain as these units reach the end of their life span.
  47|                                   In conclusion, I would like to thank the members of the Lunenburg Fire Department for their outstanding commitment to
  48|                       the Town. Their enthusiasm, professionalism and dedication continue to impress me every day. I would also like to thank the
  49|                        other department heads, boards and commissions for their continuing help. Most people do not realize the level of
  50|                       interdepartmental cooperation that occurs in the daily course of town business. My thanks also go out to the citizens of
  51|                       Lunenburg for their ongoing support of public safety. It is that support that allows us to continue providing the services you
  52|                        expect from us.
  53|                                                                                               80

   2|                       I would like to take this opportunity to thank the Citizens of Lunenburg for making the second annual
   3|                       Citizen's Police Academy a success. I am again grateful for the enthusiastic support of the Citizen
   4|                       Students. The program curriculum included: Criminal Law, Court Process, Firearms Familiarization,
   5|                       Use of Force and Defensive Tactics, Taser, Traffic Safety & Accident Investigation, K-9 Operations,
   6|                       Crime Scene Photography, Drug Investigations, and Latent Finger Print Recovery.
   7|                       I have nothing but praise the officers who volunteered their time to present these subjects; Dep. Chief
   8|                       Retired, Paul Bozicas, Clerk Magistrate Patrick Malone, Sgt. Sean Connery, Sgt. Tom Gammel, Officer
   9|                       Brad McNamara, Officer Josh Tocci and Det. Sgt. Jeff Thibodeau. A special thanks again this year to
  10|                       Lieutenant Mike Luth for organizing and facilitating the program. The next Citizen's academy will be
  11|                       scheduled in the fall of 2018. I look forward to seeing you then.
  12|                                                                       About the Police Department
  13|                        The Police Department is in operation 24 hours a day 7 days a week. Two patrol officers are assigned to
  14|                       each shift with support from the Traffic Enforcement/K-9 Officer who works two day shifts and two
  15|                       evening shifts. Also supporting the patrol unit is the Criminal Investigation Unit headed by a Detective
  16|                       Sergeant. The Community Outreach officer, who is also assigned as the School Resource Officer comes
  17|                       under the auspices of the C.I.U. Unit The Lieutenant and Public Safety Coordinator are on duty Monday
  18|                       thru Friday between the hours of 7Am and 3Pm. Office hours for the Chief of Police are 8Am to 4Pm
  19|                       Monday thru Friday. The Animal Control Officer, who is under auspices of the Police Department, is on
  20|                       call.
  21|                       The front desk is open Monday thru Friday from 7AM to 7PM. Emergency, Non-emergency and 911
  22|                       calls are routed through Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch District, (NVRDD). For all emergencies dial
  23|                       911. For general calls please call (978) 582-4531.
  24|                        Applications for Firearms Licensing can be found on the Police Department Website at
  25|                       lunenburgma.gov. Contact (978) 582-4531 for an appointment.
  26|                                                                                               85

===PAGE 86=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|                       The public is reminded to report any animal related complaints to the Regional Call Center at (978)
   2|                        582-4531. For emergencies dial 911. More information can be found on the Animal Control Web-site at
   3|                       lunenburgma.gov.
   4|                        Dogs must be licensed by April 1st annually. Dogs must have a current Rabies certificate to qualify for
   5|                       licensing. This can be done at the Clerk's office in Town Hall, 17 Main Street Lunenburg, MA 01462.
   6|                        Unspayed Female/Unaltered Male $20 fee. Spayed Female/Altered Male $5 fee. Late Fee: $25
   7|                                                                  The State of the Police Department
   8|                        The Police responded to 17048 calls for service this year, a 2.2% increase over last year; responded to
   9|                       301 motor vehicle accidents, investigated 1282 incidents, 44 Field Interview Reports, wrote 2236 motor
  10|                       vehicle citations, arrested 193 people, summonsed 175, and placed 13 people in protective custody. The
  11|                       K-9 was deployed 27 times. In the first half of fiscal 2017 staff assignments had to be increased 75
  12|                       times to effectively meet service demands.
  13|                       The day shift desk officer answered 2083 calls and took 1402 lobby inquiries and complaints between
  14|                       July and December of 2017. The day shift desk officer wrote 93 incident reports and 3 field interview
  15|                       reports in 2017. This year the day shift desk watch officer was responsible for conducting suicide watch
  16|                       and assisting with the court processing of 66 detainees; either taken into custody overnight or during the
  17|                       day shift. The day shift desk officer processed 134 Firearms Licenses, 11 Non FID related finger prints,
  18|                       and registered 13 Sex Offenders. The 3pm to 7pm desk watch officers assisted in the processing of, 50
  19|                       detainees while providing suicide watch and numerous other services as noted in the job description
  20|                       below.
  21|                       I would like to take this opportunity to thank the Town for supporting the staff increase that I requested
  22|                       in the FY 18 budget. The addition of one officer enabled the Police Department to create a School
  23|                        Resource Officer Position. An outpouring of input from the community, and the shift in societal values
  24|                       and dynamic social change has combined to influence the decision to create such an important job.
  25|                        Growth revenue provided the funding for the additional officer and although the School Resource
  26|                        Officer position is important to ease the concerns of crime and violence in schools, because the Officer
  27|                       is committed to the School District for at least 10 months, it does not help the Police Department keep
  28|                       pace with the demands for service. This year, although arrests are down, calls for service continue to rise
  29|                       causing the work load to grow.
  30|                       In the past twenty years the police department has not seen any significant increase in staffing despite a
  31|                       significant increase in community development and growth. For several years I have asked for three
  32|                       officers per year until the police department reaches an appropriate number of full time officers to meet
  33|                       the work load demand. I have proven through work load demand studies that this police department
  34|                       should employ between twenty-three (23) and twenty-six (26) officers. The studies agree with
  35|                        Department of Justice assessments which indicate that based upon our population the Lunenburg Police
  36|                       Department should employ 2.6 officers per thousand populations.
  37|                       In past presentations I have publicized town reports dating back to 1996 that makes it clear the number
  38|                       one priority has been to increase the size of the police department. Recently I discovered a letter from
  39|                       the previous police chief addressed to the then town manager dated April 23, 2006. The letter indicated
  40|                       that the police department employed 13 full time officers including the Chief of Police, today it employs
  41|                       15. The letter pointed out that a survey conducted by the Bureau of Justice Assistance revealed that 21
  42|                       full time police officers for every 10,000 residents served. The Police Chief reports in the letter that a
  43|                       Massachusetts Police Employment Survey revealed an average of 2.1 police officers per 1000 residents
  44|                       served with some 3.0 and the lowest 1.8 officers per thousand residents served. Attached to this letter
  45|                                                                                               86

===PAGE 87=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|                       was a local police officer staffing survey which revealed an average population of 12,200 had a staffing
   2|                       level of 21 officers. Most of the towns in the 10000 population range had 18, 19, 21 full time officers
   3|                       employed.
   4|                       I will be repeating myself once again with a request for staffing increases in the FY19 budget. I remind
   5|                       those who have control over the budget that the service the public expects will continue to decline due to
   6|                       inadequate employment policies over a long period of time. Once again I will take a quote from last
   7|                       year's budget memo, "the time to properly investigate crimes, solve problems by engaging the
   8|                       community, or providing a quick emergency response is continually at odds with the demands of
   9|                        growth. The community will eventually see the police department as a useless ineffective service that is
  10|                       a drain on their income and their support for the Police will decline." Again, knowing the challenges
  11|                       just to approach level funding, e.g. contractual pay increases, net increases in supplies, etc., I encourage
  12|                       you to fund an incremental increase in staffing over the next several years utilizing growth revenue.
  13|                        Calls for service have increased 2.2% over last year and 32.5% over the past five years. The Police
  14|                       responded to 301 Motor Vehicle Accidents, investigated 1282 incidents, 44 Field interview reports,
  15|                        wrote 2236 motor vehicle violations, arrested 193 people, summonsed 175 and placed 13 people in
  16|                       protective custody. The K-9 was deployed 27 times. In the first half of fiscal 2017 staff assignments had
  17|                       to be increased 75 times to effectively meet service demands.

  11|                                                                       Arrests
  12|                       Incidents and arrests declined this year but are still on the rise over the past five and ten years. Calls for
  13|                       service increased which may account for the decrease in arrests. Crime is down this year however;
  14|                       crimes against the person are down 7%, property crimes are down 16%, drug and gun crimes are down
  15|                       41%, and group B crimes, e.g. all other offenses, check fraud, drunk driving, disorderly conduct,
  16|                       trespassing, liquor law violations are down by 26%.
  17|                       Calls for service will continue to rise as the community grows. Crime is unpredictable--a moving target-
  18|                       - which makes budgeting very challenging. A single catastrophe and can wipe out a police overtime
  19|                       budget. Being prepared, however, is the key to a successful law enforcement agency and I can't
  20|                       emphasize enough how important staffing is with respect to being adequately prepared. The community
  21|                       has to be willing to support the cost of preparedness.
  22|                       I can't emphasize enough how important it is to keep with current growth trends with respect to staffing
  23|                       the police department. Growth, historically plays a major role in calls for service, arrests, incident and
  24|                       motor vehicle accidents. The demands for service will continue to rise over time. I respectfully ask for
  25|                       your support with funding request so that the Police Department can keep up with the demands of
  26|                       growth and offer the service the public expects and is entitled to.
  27|                       I thank you again for allowing me to manage your Law Enforcement agency another year. I appreciate
  28|                       all citizen input and value the support of the public. Please be diligent about reporting crimes. Let's
  29|                       embrace the concept of, "see something, say something."
  30|                       Respectfully Submitted
  31|                       Chief James P. Marino

   1|                          Lunenburg's Charter specifically mandates a Capital Planning Committee prepare a Fiscal Year Plan and Capital
   2|                         Improvement Plan to be presented to the Town Manager. The criterion for any capital request includes mandates,
   3|                         safety, need, crisis, cost effectiveness and ability to fund. The committee has to review each request under these
   4|                         six elements. Not only do they have to determine that the request fits within the criteria, but they have to prioritize
   5|                         the level that meets the criteria. Therefore, not all requests are included in the fiscal year plans, mostly due to
   6|                         funding.
   7|                         At the Annual Town Meeting in May 2017, the following items totaling $633,317, were
   8|                         presented in the FY18 plan.

  10|                                                                                Zoning Board of Appeals
  11|                              The Zoning Board of Appeals heard petitions during calendar year 2017. There were several applicants seeking special
  12|                              permits and special permits for dimensional variations. The Zoning Board meetings are held on the second and fourth
  13|                              Wednesdays of the month on an "as needed" basis.
  14|                              The Zoning Board consists of the following members: Alfred Gravelle, Chairman, James Besarkarski, Vice Chairman,
  15|                              Members: Robert Cadle, Paul Doherty and Hans Wentrup and Associate members, David Blatt, and Sheila Lumi. Lisa
  16|                              Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Zoning Board.
  17|                              The functions of your Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statures are as follows:
  18|                                   1. To hear and decide applications for Variances from Town's Protective By-Laws as authorized in the By-laws.
  19|                                   2. To hear and decide applications for Special Permits as authorized in the Town's Protective By-laws.
  20|                                   3. To hear and decide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts General Laws, as
  21|                                       amended, from any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain a permit or enforcement action
  22|                                        from the Administrative Officer or Board in the Town, the Regional Planning Agency, abutting City or Town or
  23|                                        other Administrative Official.
  24|                                   4. To act upon application for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General Laws, Chapter
  25|                                       40B. The Zoning Board of Appeals reviews requests for comprehensive permits submitted to the Board under
  26|                                        Chapter 40B by developers who wish to create affordable housing. These requests can, by law, circumvent local
  27|                                        zoning ordinances.
  28|                              The office of the Zoning Board of Appeals is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts Avenue,
  29|                              Lunenburg. Business hours are 8:00 AM to 4:00 PM Monday and Wednesday and 8:00 AM to 6:00 PM Tuesday and
  30|                              Thursday, closed Friday. If you need any assistance from the Zoning Board of Appeals please contact us at 978-582-4146
  31|                               X 421 during regular business hours.            Zoning Board applications are available in the office and online at
  32|                              www.Lunenburgonline.gov.

   1|                                                                          Lunenburg Public Schools
   2|                                                                                        2017-2018
   3|                       Through its stated Mission and Core Values, The Lunenburg Public Schools provides its
   4|                       students with an understanding of, and a responsible commitment to the principles of'
   5|                       freedom, respect, personal worth and a concern for each other.
   6|                       The Mission of the Lunenburg Public Schools is to provide all students with the skills,
   7|                       confidence and passion for life-long learning so that they may find their own paths to
   8|                       successful careers, active citizenship and rewarding lives.
   9|                       The Core Values of the Lunenburg Public Schools
  10|                                   • Quality education cultivates creative problem solving, independent thinking
  11|                                      and multiple points of view.
  12|                                   • Effective education adresses the whole child, involve authentic engagement,
  13|                                      and provide multiple pathways to success.
  14|                                   • Students must be provided with an academically and personally safe
  15|                                      environment that promotes effective learning.
  16|                                   • Students should be provided with opportunities to develop initiative and
  17|                                      responsibility which will help them become active and supportive community
  18|                                       members.
  19|                                   • Focused, sustained professional development and reflective practice are
  20|                                      fundamental to excellence in teaching.
  21|                                   • Strong collaboration and open communication among school staff, students,
  22|                                     families and the community develop mutual respect and trust, and enhance
  23|                                     students' ability to succeed
  24|                                   • Resourcing decisions should be responsible, address critical needs and put
  25|                                     students first.
