  19|                                                                          Superintendent's Message
  20|                        I am pleased to report another engaging and productive year of teaching and learning in
  21|                        the Lunenburg Public Schools. Typically I would review our state assessment results in
  22|                       this report, but our District did not receive an accountability rating this year since
  23|                        students in grades 3-8 participated in 2017 Next Generation MCAS tests. Students
  24|                       received individual results and the high school received the highest accountability
  25|                       ranking. The next-generation MCAS is a reformatted test, and as such the scores are not
  26|                        comparable to the prior tests. The next-generation MCAS emphasizes readiness for
  27|                        higher-level work at the next grade level and has different performance categories.
  28|                        To support the students our faculty engaged in focused professional development and
  29|                        curriculum renewal to ensure alignment with the new rigorous State standards and
  30|                        assessments consistent with the new strategic plan. After a year-long development effort
  31|                        involving the entire community, in the fall of 2017 the School Committee adopted a new
  32|                        mission, vision and strategic plan, based upon the core values identified in the planning
  33|                        process.
  34|                        Strategic Plan 2018-2023
  35|                        Goal 1: Social-emotional learning, prevention programs and secure schools provide safe
  36|                                    environments, promote kindness and celebrate cultural differences.
  37|                        Goal 2: Civic learning and engagement enable all students to become knowledgeable
  38|                                    citizens and active community members.
  39|                        Goal 3: Curricula and assessments in major subject areas are aligned with recent
  40|                                    Massachusetts State Frameworks and fully implemented.
  41|                                                                                               97

===PAGE 98=== (ocr, 44 lines)
   1|                       Goal 4: Implementation of digital literacy and computer science curriculum is expanded;
   2|                                   and, instructional technologies are more available.
   3|                       Goal 5: The processes for annual professional development ensure opportunities for
   4|                                   well-targeted individual, school-based, and district-wide professional growth.
   5|                       Goal 6: Strategies to improve instructional support for struggling learners are enhanced.
   6|                       Goal 7: Enrichment opportunities are expanded to include the arts and other areas of high
   7|                       student interest.
   8|                       Goal 8: Collaboration and communications within and among the schools and the
   9|                       community are improved.
  10|                       The first year update on progress toward the above goals will be posted on the District
  11|                       website in June 2018.
  12|                       We are in the process of closing out a very successful building project. The Lunenburg
  13|                       Middle-High School finished on time. The approved projected project budget was
  14|                       $72,590,848. At the time of writing this report, the project is projected to come in
  15|                       approximately $4,477,355 under the projected budget.
  16|                       This fall we opened all the new grass fields. Coupled with the refurbished track and field
  17|                       facility, this outdoor facility is second to none in the region. Our coaches and athletes
  18|                       were excited for the opportunity to practice and play on-site after the loss of most of the
  19|                       fields during construction. This fall we also welcomed to our school campus Lunenburg
  20|                       youth and adult sports teams. Our Adult Education/Lifelong Learning program continues
  21|                       to grow and expand. Five new activities were added to the program this year including
  22|                       educational presentations on the opioid crisis offered in conjunction with the Lunenburg
  23|                       Resources for Opioid Awareness and Recovery Task Force (ROAR). The partnership
  24|                       between the School Department, the Lifelong Learning Program, and the youth sports
  25|                       programs is an example of the positive outcome of a community working together to
  26|                       meet the needs of all its citizens.
  27|                       Abigail L. Dwyer received the 2017 Superintendent's Award. Ms. Dwyer is the daughter
  28|                       of Todd and Jacqueline Dwyer. During her four years at LHS Abigail enrolled in a
  29|                       rigorous course of studies taking honors and advanced placement courses.                                                            She was
  30|                       active in the arts as a vocalist and thespian. Ms. Dwyer was also active as a volunteer in
  31|                       her school and community while also working part-time. In college she will study
  32|                       sociology and mathematics and then plans to attend law school. I was honored to
  33|                       recognize this accomplished young woman.
  34|                       I am extremely pleased to announce our affiliation with the North Central Massachusetts
  35|                       Early Literacy Innovation Zone. The Lunenburg Public Schools in conjunction with the
  36|                       Community Foundation of North Central Massachusetts, the United Way of North
  37|                       Central Massachusetts, Fitchburg State University, Footsteps2Brilliance and nine other
  38|                       districts in the area have joined together to create a collective impact, region-wide
  39|                       literacy to address school readiness with the goal of all students reading on grade level by
  40|                       third grade. Two applications are available to any family residing in Lunenburg at no
  41|                       cost. Using mobile technology, families have access to a comprehensive curriculum of
  42|                       books, songs, and games in English and Spanish. The curriculum can be downloaded and
  43|                       access off line. We are excited to be part of this regional effort. Information about how
  44|                                                                                               98

===PAGE 99=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|                       to access the programs is available by calling the Superintendent's Office or at the
   2|                       District website.
   3|                       This past year our schools bid farewell to retirees who devoted many years to public
   4|                       service.          Retiring in 2017 were: Sandra Curley, Susan Diamantopoulos, Steve
   5|                       Kyajohnian, Claudette LaChance, Joanne Londa, Marie Martin, Sharon McCullah, Lisa
   6|                       Stone, and Cathy Zayka. Added together these employees have 196 years of service to

   8|                       each of them our heartfelt thanks and sincere appreciation for their service. At this time I
   9|                       also want to extend my thanks for the honor of serving the Lunenburg students and

  11|                       Lunenburg, 15 as Superintendent. A special thanks to my assistants during my tenure
  12|                       Liz Petersen and Mary Landi. I am grateful for the opportunity to work with so many

   9|                                                                                 School Facilities
  10|                       The first year of operation of the new Lunenburg Middle-High School included training
  11|                       on the new systems in the school, discovering warranty items that needed to be addressed
  12|                       and collecting data on the school operating cost. The school relies heavily on technology
  13|                       to operate efficiently and the maintenance staff continued to learn about the heating and
  14|                       ventilation controls, lighting system, security camera and door access system, telephone
  15|                       system and fire safety system throughout the year. Between the various contractors and
  16|                       the school maintenance personnel mostly all issues were rapidly resolved.                                                                   One
  17|                       significant finding in late February 2017 was that snow sliding on the pitched roofs
  18|                       toward the center of the A and D wings had the potential to do great damage to root top
  19|                       equipment and to roof penetrations. In July 2017, roof guards were added to the A and D
  20|                       building by Greenwood Industries at selected locations to protect those items that were
  21|                       felt to be at risk from sliding snow. An unresolved issue with rooftop ventilation units
  22|                       that caused the units to shut down at start up during temperatures below 20 degrees,
  23|                       continues to be worked on by the contractors and equipment manufacturer and is
  24|                       expected to be resolved by the end of March 2018.
  25|                       The roof top solar project for Lunenburg Middle-High School was completed in June
  26|                       2017 which will produce 335,000 kilowatt hours in the first year of operation at a fixed
  27|                       price for the next 20 years at .83 cents per kilowatt. This represents an estimated 40% of
  28|                       the school electricity needs. This roof top unit coupled with two power purchasing
  29|                       agreements through the town lead to a significant reduction in cost to operate the school
  30|                       and resulted in all electricity needs for the school district being provided by solar power.
  31|                       The focus of school maintenance for FY18 continues on preventative maintenance on
  32|                       heating and ventilation systems and energy conservation measures. Funding for
  33|                       conversion of Turkey Hill Elementary School to LED lighting and building envelope
  34|                       tightening will be high priorities for Lunenburg Schools in the March 2018 Green
  35|                       Communities Competitive Grant round.
  36|                       The FY 18 capital program funded additional asbestos abatement at Turkey Hill
  37|                       Elementary School. In July and August 2017, Encore Contracting Services for $168,079
  38|                       removed spray-on fire proofing above the ceiling in the first floor hallway and one
  39|                       stairwell of the classroom wing. This project included removal of the splined ceiling and
  40|                       the floor tiles in the hallway, as well as the flooring in the administration area and the
  41|                       connecting corridors. Turkey Hill Elementary School is expected to continue to have
  42|                       additional asbestos removal planned for a number of years.
  43|                       Respectfully submitted,
  44|                       John Londa, Director of Facilities and Grounds
  45|                                                                                              100

===PAGE 101=== (ocr, 42 lines)
   1|                                                                            Technology Department
   2|                       Technology touches all our lives every day at the Lunenburg Public Schools. It is our aim
   3|                       in IT to serve our students, faculty, and staff proactively, so that the tools used to
   4|                       facilitate learning function seamlessly and responsively when needed. From checking
   5|                       email, to accessing class information, to connecting and collaborating with colleagues
   6|                       near and far, faculty, staff, and IT are working together to help redefine what is possible
   7|                       for our students, so they can redefine what's possible for the world. Our annual report
   8|                       highlights the IT team, as well as some of the improvements we have made this past year
   9|                       to achieve this goal.
  10|                       The IT Team
  11|                        Our team continues to learn and grow each year as we improve our infrastructure and
  12|                       explore innovations in education technology.
  13|                       Steve Malandrinos - Information Technology Director
  14|                             • Five years working for the Town and Schools
  15|                             • Previously an engineer at Cisco Systems, IT Director for the Town of
  16|                                    Belchertown, MA and Belchertown Public Schools
  17|                        Daniel Nadareski - Network Administrator
  18|                             • Retired Police Officer from the Albany, NY police force with over 24 years on
  19|                                   the job
  20|                             • Prior experience includes computer crime and forensics, network administration,
  21|                                    video security system administration, and IP phone system administration
  22|                       Nicholas Scoggins - School Technology Coordinator
  23|                             • Received Associate and Bachelor of Science degrees from ITT Technical Institute
  24|                             • Served 5 years as Security System Administrator at Greater Lawrence Technical
  25|                                    School
  26|                       Jordan Pochini - Tech Support Specialist
  27|                             • Graduated from Fitchburg State University with a degree in Computer Science
  28|                             • Prior experience includes both individual contributor and management roles in
  29|                                   retail environments
  30|                       Spurred on by the need for capacity for computer based testing, our student-to-device
  31|                       ratio continues to improve year after year. We strive to put as many devices into
  32|                       students' hands as possible, with the goal of making the improvements sustainable in
  33|                       following years always top of mind. In the spring of 2016, our districtwide student-to-
  34|                       device ratio was approximately 4.2 to 1. As of now, it stands around 1.4 to 1 and
  35|                       continues to drop each year due to district-funded improvements as well as generous
  36|                       donations from individuals and organizations like the PTO.
  37|                       Our ultimate goal remains to provide the tools necessary for faculty, staff, and students
  38|                       alike to facilitate the learning process in a secure, highly-available, and high-performing
  39|                       environment, and to always just 'make IT work'.
  40|                       Respectfully Submitted,
  41|                       Steve Malandrinos, Information Technology Director

   3|                                    From the March 1, 2017 report, there are 275 students with disabilities receiving
   4|                        special education or related services in Lunenburg (last year there were 299). This
   5|                        represents 16.9% of our total student population of 1,628 students (it was 18.8% last
   6|                        year). The state average from the 2017 school report card is 17.4%. As of March 1,
   7|                        2017, the total number of students receiving services outside the district was 14 (last year
   8|                        was 19). Of the 14 students, 2 are placed in Collaborative Placements, 8 are in Day
   9|                        Placements and 4 are in Residential Placements. Additionally, from the March 1, 2017
  10|                        report, 103 students identified with a disability received accommodations via 504 plans in
  11|                        Lunenburg (last year was 114).
  12|                                     This year as a district, our overall Accountability and Assistance Level is NO
  13|                        LEVEL with the Primary School (Insufficient data), Turkey Hill Elementary School (No
  14|                        level), Lunenburg Middle School (No level), and Lunenburg High School (Level 1). The
  15|                        state report card lists the district's determination of need for Special Education technical
  16|                        Requirements - At Risk (MRAR)". Students with Disabilities participated in MCAS this
  17|                        year with the following rates: English Language Arts = 99% (it was 99% last year),
  18|                        Mathematics = 99% (It was 100% last year), and Science = 97% (It was 100% last year).

===PAGE 104=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|                       Indicator 12 -Early Childhood Transition. This indicator measures the % of children
   2|                       referred by Part C, found eligible for special education services, and who have an IEP
   3|                       developed and implemented by their third birthdays. The state target for this indicator in
   4|                       100% and Lunenburg is noted as "Suppressed". Data is suppressed if the number of
   5|                       students referred by Part C and found eligible for special education services is fewer than
   6|                       10. In Lunenburg, only 2 students were referred during 2012-2013 (we have not been
   7|                       reviewed since 2012-2013 for updated data in this area.
   8|                       Indicator 14 - Post-School Outcomes for Students with IEPs. This indicator measures
   9|                       the % of students with IEPs who exited high school during the 2014-15 school year and
  10|                       self-reported post-school engagement in education or employment one year after leaving
  11|                       high school. Engagement is defined through three measurements:
  12|                             • Measurement A: Enrolled in higher education within one year of leaving high
  13|                                   school.
  14|                                   Measurement B: Enrolled in higher education or competitively employed within
  15|                                   one year of leaving high school.
  16|                             • Measurement C: Enrolled in higher education, or in some other postsecondary
  17|                                   education or training program; or competitively employed, or in some other
  18|                                   employment within one year of leaving high school.
  19|                       Measurements are cumulative with Measurement C reflecting the total number of exiters
  20|                       engaged in a post-secondary activity one year after leaving high school. For the 2015-
  21|                       2016 school year, state targets for the three measurements of Post-School Outcomes are
  22|                       Measurement A: 49%; Measurement B: 84%; and Measurement C: 91%. Lunenburg
  23|                       rates are as follows (from reporting year 2014-15): Measurement A = 50%,
  24|                       Measurement B = 75%, and Measurement C = 75%.
  25|                                   The Parent Advisory Council Supporting All Learners (PACSAL) continues to be
  26|                       an active support group for parents, teachers and administration. All parents and
  27|                       community members can join the group, which currently consists of parents of students
  28|                       receiving Title I services and parents of students with disabilities. Meetings are typically
  29|                       held the first Friday of each month, 11:00 A.M., in the Planning Room at the Middle
  30|                       School. The executive board is made up of Chairperson: Kelly Harvey. Several events
  31|                       and presentations were organized by the PACSAL for both parents and teachers in
  32|                       Lunenburg this year. For more information, go to:
  33|                       https://sites.google.com/a/lunenburgschools.net/special-services/pacsal
  34|                                   In conclusion, the Lunenburg Public Schools continues to provide a variety of
  35|                       special needs services to meet the varying needs of our students in the least restrictive
  36|                       environment. We have an experienced, hard working staff that works together with
  37|                       parents to provide these varied services. Our parents are equal partners in our efforts to
  38|                       develop challenging and focused educational programs for children.                                                            We remain
  39|                       committed to the Lunenburg Public Schools mission, "The mission of the Lunenburg
  40|                       Public Schools is to provide all students with the skills, confidence, and passion for life-
  41|                       long learning so that they may find their own paths to successful careers, active
  42|                       citizenship, and rewarding lives." This is a total community effort and we encourage
  43|                       ALL Lunenburg residents to join with us to create positive experiences and futures for

  16|                       event, large or small.

  18|                       the open houses this year, we answered many questions and received lots of feedback.
  19|                       Our School Lunch Program web page has been updated with information about lunch
  20|                       prices, online payments and more information about our lunch program. This page can be
  21|                       reached by clicking on the • School Lunch program link which is available on each of
  22|                       our schools websites. I would like to encourage parents if you have not done so to create
  23|                       an account on PowerSchool. Doing so is a great way to monitor and set low balance
  24|                       alerts on student lunch accounts. The lunch menus for all of our cafeterias can be found
  25|                       on each school's website under the Lunch Menu link.
  26|                       Our newest endeavor this year is very exciting. Our Primary School cafeteria has been
  27|                       selected to participate in the Massachusetts Smarter Lunchroom Movement. The
  28|                       Massachusetts Movement is collaboration between the John Stalker Institute and the
  29|                       Department of Elementary and Secondary Education. For more information please visit
  30|                       https://www.smarterlunchrooms.org/about. On Thursday February 8* our Smarter
  31|                       Lunchrooms coach came out to the Primary School to observe and assess our lunch
  32|                       service. She found that we are doing many things to gently nudge our students to make
  33|                       healthy choices giving us a bronze level on our score. The Primary Cafeteria staff looks
  34|                       forward to serving our school community healthful appetizing meals.

  37|                                                                        Lunenburg Primary School
  38|                              The Lunenburg Primary School is an early childhood education center for students
  39|                       Preschool to 2nd           grade. Our students range from three years old to eight years old. Our
  40|                       community is made up of students, parents, teachers and support staff; working together
  41|                       to create an educationally rich and safe learning environment that focuses on the whole
  42|                                                                                              105

===PAGE 106=== (ocr, 44 lines)
   1|                       The October 1, 2017 student count reported to the Department of Elementary and
   2|                       Secondary Education was three hundred and ninety-seven students. Our Preschool
   3|                       program services forty-two three and four year old students. We have five Kindergarten
   4|                       classes servicing one hundred and nine students. Class sizes in our Kindergartens are at
   5|                       21 to 22 students. This past year we added a 1st                               grade teacher in order to keep class sizes
   6|                       to 20-21. We have one hundred and twenty 1st                                    grade students. First grade is a critical
   7|                       learning year for Reading and Writing and we feel that lowering class sizes will allow us
   8|                       to focus on individual student learning. Second grade has one hundred and twenty- three
   9|                       students with class sizes ranging from 24 to 25 students.
  10|                            Our professional development this year centered on Responsive Classroom and
  11|                       Emotional Intelligence for the second year. All of our classroom teachers are working on
  12|                       improving the use of Responsive Classroom Learning Curriculum which includes
  13|                       morning meetings, guided discovery, rules, logical consequences and academic choice.
  14|                            To strengthen our commitment to focusing on the whole child and the Social
  15|                       Emotional Learning (SEL) needs of our children, teachers and staff have received
  16|                       professional development on Responsive classroom and Emotional Intelligence. Mrs.
  17|                       Nancy Chapdelaine has led our educators through in-service lessons for the second year
  18|                       in a row focusing this year on Rules in School. This year RULER was rolled in the
  19|                       classroom. Students and their families are learning about Emotional Intelligence, they
  20|                       have developed Charters to live and work by, instituted Mood Meters to assist students to
  21|                       learn more about emotions and the strategies to use to shift to a more productive place for
  22|                       learning. They have learned about a meta-moment and a blue print for conflict resolution.
  23|                            We are dedicated to making the Lunenburg Primary School one that focuses on
  24|                       helping our community of students, staff and parents understand their emotions and how
  25|                       we bring our best selves to the learning we do each and every day. Information on these
  26|                       two          SEL's            can         be        found           at       our         website            Or     by going to
  27|                       https://www.responsiveclassroom.org/and http://ei.yale.edu/
  28|                            The Primary School is a Title One school and we have continued our tiered
  29|                       instructional system to insure that all students have the services they need to be
  30|                       successful. We have a growing English Learning Population of 2.3% of our student
  31|                       population; Thirty-five percent of our students are considered to be high needs students
  32|                       according to the Department of Elementary and Secondary Education, and 17.6% are
  33|                       economically disadvantaged students. Common assessments are completed in the fall,
  34|                       winter, and spring for all children in grades K-2 in the areas of reading, writing, and
  35|                       math.          These common assessments serve to monitor the effectiveness of teacher
  36|                       instruction and drive individual student instruction. Struggling students are identified
  37|                       quickly and additional resources and instruction are provided to address the areas of
  38|                       concern identified. Tiered instructional services are provided in the area of Language
  39|                       Arts and Math. The tiered instructional model requires that the teachers not only meet
  40|                       the needs of the students who may be struggling with learning but those students who are
  41|                       doing well and need to achieve higher levels of learning. Learning is more personalized
  42|                       and students are being challenged to be confident independent learners and problem
  43|                       solvers.
  44|                                                                                              106

===PAGE 107=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|                             Our staff works hard to maintain a school culture which is positive, inclusive, and
   2|                        supportive of each of our members. We have high expectations, insist on respectful
   3|                        behavior, and recognize the importance of building a community where members feel a
   4|                       sense of belonging and responsibility to contribute. Our students have maintained a high
   5|                       level of community engagement and civic learning that is fostered through service
   6|                       learning projects at each grade level. The Kindergarten students learn about our local
   7|                       food bank and gift mart and sponsor school-wide can and gift drives through-out the year.
   8|                       Our 2nd grade students have learned about our local veterans and organized Valentines to
   9|                        go to area Veteran's Hospitals. Students in the First grade have committed their energy
  10|                        to support our local animal shelters. For the last several years we participate in Trick-or-
  11|                       Treat for UNICEF as a school, collecting a little over four hundred and fifty-two dollars.
  12|                            The Primary School Parent/Teacher Advisory Council meets each month to discuss
  13|                       school related concerns and activities. They review the annual budget for the school and
  14|                       provide advice for the administration on the school's parent handbook and School
  15|                       Improvement Plan.

  35|                       for nine years and as the Principal of the Lunenburg Primary School for six years. I will

  37|                        success and life-long learning. I look forward to this new chapter in my life as I enter
  38|                        retirement.

  29|                      Professional development has focused on aligning the curriculum across all subject areas
  30|                      so that students will have common experiences at each grade level. We continue to
  31|                      refine the Teacher Support Team protocol, which examines struggling students'
  32|                      academic/personal histories and focuses on the best interventions. Our aim is not only to
  33|                      support a teacher's current concerns, but also to streamline access to important
  34|                      background information and most successful strategies for next year's teachers. It is
  35|                      imperative to identify where students begin the year academically and catapult them
  36|                      forward as far as educationally sound strategies can bring them to the end of the year. All
  37|                      students are assessed using the Developmental Reading Assessment (DRA) and Aimsweb
  38|                       monitor individual student progress. We rely in part upon the expertise of our full time
  39|                      reading specialist, who works with Title 1 students and provides support and consultation
  40|                       for classroom teachers.
  41|                       We acknowledge that social learning in a supportive community is as important to
  42|                      success as academic learning. To that end, we embraced a CARES motto, which focuses
  43|                      on Civility, Achievement, Respectfulness, Effort, and Sportsmanship. Every grade level
  44|                                                                                               110

===PAGE 111=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|                         team has created a student learning and professional practice goal related to teaching,
   2|                         modeling, and promoting civility as an attribute of lifelong value. They have developed
   3|                         lessons to promote civility between peers, with adults, and in families. With the full time
   4|                         services of our guidance counselor, many students meet weekly in small groups to
   5|                         develop both social skills and strategies to cope with anxiety, focus issues, and academic
   6|                         challenges. We facilitate the opportunity for outside counselors to meet with students
   7|                         one to one. We host the Big Sibs, Little Sibs program on Wednesday afternoons for high
   8|                         school students to make connections with younger students in a structured activity group.
   9|                         We again hosted the TIGER program from Plymouth State University that focuses on a
  10|                         positive message of inclusiveness and how to advocate for yourself and others. We also
  11|                         engage students through broader community experiences such as Nature's Classroom for
  12|                         fifth grade, the Boston Museum of Science programs that visit both fourth and third
  13|                         grade, academically themed tield trips to the Concord Museum or the Southwick Loo,
  14|                         after school activities, the Girls on the Run program to promote the empowerment of our
  15|                         girls, band for fifth grade and chorus for all three grades. The Bruins Be Fit School
  16|                         Assembly Program is coming next March, a 45-minute all-school program focused on
  17|                         promoting a happy, healthy lifestyle by emphasizing the importance of proper nutrition,
  18|                         perseverance, and physical activity through the sport of hockey.
  19|                         We are continuing to develop a healthy, successful school community with a foundation
  20|                         of open communication and sound operational practices. Our leadership team consists of
  21|                         reps from each grade level, special areas, and special services. The members are both
  22|                         conduits of communication between administration and staff and decision makers about
  23|                         the direction of our school's culture and priorities. Likewise, the School Advisory
  24|                         Council, composed of parents, community members, teachers and a school committee
  25|                         representative, also provide input and influence the school's culture and priorities. The
  26|                         principal's and assistant principal's role as instructional leader is part of the equation as
  27|                         well. Providing effective feedback to teachers around the teaching and learning is an
  28|                         essential element for student success.
  29|                         Education is a community partnership. It is the support of the larger community that
  30|                         enriches the learning opportunities for students and staff. We are so grateful for all of our
  31|                         generous supporters: the PTO whose funds allow all students to participate in field trips
  32|                         and supported the purchase of a new Chromebook cart, private donations for technology,
  33|                         the Lunenburg Lions Club who provided scholarships for Nature's Classroom and
  34|                         regularly provides food donations for students at Extended Day, BoxTop collectors and
  35|                         counters, Hannaford Helps supporters, Community Reader day volunteers, parents (both
  36|                         those who support their children at home as well as those who donate hours to school,
  37|                         North End Mazda for their donation of dozens of science books, our Veteran's Day and
  38|                         Memorial Day guests and guest speakers.

   1|                                                                           Lunenburg Middle School
   2|                          Lunenburg Middle School provides a rigorous academic curriculum as well as strong
   3|                          programs that support students' social and emotional growth. These programs ensure that
   4|                          each child becomes a self-motivated, creative, lifelong learner in a safe, stimulating
   5|                          environment that encourages diversity and open honest communication. The dedicated
   6|                          and accomplished staff of professionals within the school embraces the philosophy of
   7|                          educating the whole child academically, socially, and emotionally. This philosophy is a
   8|                          means by which all students become positive community members that possess the skills
   9|                          to be successful in high school. Lunenburg Middle School is in its second year as a co-
  10|                          located middle- high school serving grades six through eight in a beautiful new facility.
  11|                          Middle School students have experienced some new opportunities with the new middle
  12|                          school, such as foreign language and foreign language exploratory for all grade levels as
  13|                          school are participating in a program called Genius Hour. Genius Hour was developed to
  14|                                                % of Students scoring in the meeting or exceeding expectations
  15|                                                                              range Spring 2017 MCAS

  18|                          6                            MATH                         66%                          68                           50%
  19|                                                       ELA                          58%                          74                           50%
  20|                                                       MATH                         53%                          46                           50%

  24|                          In reviewing the results of the 2017 Spring M.C.A.S. tests, our percentage of students
  25|                          scoring in the meeting or exceeding expectations range remains strong and above the
  26|                          state average in all tested areas in the middle school. The Department of Elementary and
  27|                          Secondary Education held schools harmless of an accountability rating tor districts that
  28|                          participated in on line, next generation MCAS testing. Lunenburg Middle School
  29|                          transitional student growth scores are well above the state average and indicate high
  30|                          student growth in five of the six content areas that have student growth measured.
  31|                          Although we are proud of our students' performance, we continue to analyze data in our
  32|                          monthly results faculty meetings designed to improve our instruction for all students. Our
  33|                                                                                              113

===PAGE 114=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|                       faculty has identified areas of improvement based upon the data and implemented
   2|                       strategies to improve their instructional practices that will help students succeed.
   3|                       We continue to utilize intervention and power blocks during the school day to meet the
   4|                       needs of each individual student. These blocks of instructional time that have been built
   5|                       into our master schedule allow teachers time to work with students at their own
   6|                       individual skill level. The philosophy is to challenge students who are high academic
   7|                       achievers as well as strengthen skills for students who have identified weaknesses.
   8|                       Teachers are able to work with students in a small group to answer individual questions
   9|                       or provide more individualized support. We design this support model to allow students
  10|                       to feel more confident, thus, perform better in the classroom. We will continue to refine
  11|                       our intervention block times to best meet the needs of our students. One option for
  12|                       students during this intervention block is the use of our web based program called IXL.
  13|                       IXL allows students to work at their own skill level, build confidence and moved forward
  14|                       with work that is aligned with the state standards. IXL allows teachers a tool for true
  15|                       differentiation for every student allowing them to work and grow at their own pace and
  16|                       level.
  17|                       Lunenburg Middle School continues to offer enrichment blocks for all students.
  18|                       Enrichment classes provide students a block of time in which they receive instruction in
  19|                       the areas of developmental health and guidance. Students also receive instruction in
  20|                       physical education, art, and music classes that allows them to explore their strengths and
  21|                       talents. Students also have the opportunity to sign up for and participate in band and
  22|                       chorus during this block. This enrichment block is in addition to the traditional special
  23|                       area classes students receive, and this block offers students additional physical movement
  24|                       throughout the day, additional opportunities for hands on activities and more exposure to
  25|                       the arts, theatre and technology throughout the school day.
  26|                         rograms develope to promote social and emotional growth Ve make a positive impact on the students we serve by offering a selfawareness, and
  27|                       community service. We will continue to utilize Developmental Design program to deliver
  28|                       our social emotional curriculum. Students meet with teachers in small groups to teach
  29|                       social responsibility and problem solving, as well as developing empathy and promoting
  30|                       positive self-esteem and strong teacher/student relationships. Our social emotional
  31|                       programs have been developed to allow students to showcase their talents and abilities,
  32|                       grow and improve as individuals, and promote community outreach for the younger
  33|                       generation.
  34|                       This year, we have been able to offer foreign language exploratory to our sixth and
  35|                       seventh grade students, giving them the opportunity to explore several different foreign
  36|                       language options in order to make an informed decision on which language they would
  37|                       like to take in grade eight and high school. The addition of the foreign language
  38|                       instruction has been well received and the students enjoy exploring different cultures and
  39|                       languages.
  40|                       Lunenburg Middle School continues to provide high quality educational services and
  41|                       supports that ensure student success and promotes a passion for lifelong learning. The
  42|                       key components to our success is our talented faculty committed to making a positive
  43|                       impact on students, our parents and community who support our teaching and learning,
  44|                       and most of all, our students who inspire us through their commitment to learning.
  45|                       Together we continue to make the difference in the lives of students.
  46|                                                                                              114

  15|                                                                             Lunenburg High School
  16|                           Lunenburg High School has been a hub for academic and social activity in 2017-2018.
  17|                           Teachers continue to provide our students rigorous coursework and hold our students to
  18|                           high standards. Our students meet these challenges through collaboration, self-advocacy,
  19|                           and strong work ethic. All students in grades 10-12 have been issued a Chrome Book
  20|                           and there are wireless capabilities throughout the building. Every classroom and
  21|                          instructional space has an interactive projector and voice-lift capabilities. Our students
  22|                           are fortunate to be in a building that can meet the needs of the 215 century learner.
  23|                           During the fall sports season, our students practiced and competed on our three grass
  24|                           fields and new artificial turf field. The youth sports teams in the community were
  25|                           scheduled on the fields, as well. During the fall season, most of our fields were being
  26|                           used from 3:00 pm to 8:00 pm.
  27|                           The winter season has been very busy, as well. The gym and weight room are being used
  28|                           for a large portion of the afternoon and evening by our basketball teams, indoor track and
  29|                           field teams, and students and community members in the Adult Education Program.
  30|                           Additionally, we have athletics teams practicing and competing at: Wallace Civic Center,
  31|                           The Groton School, Nashoba Valley Ski Area, and at Fitchburg High School. Our actors
  32|                           and actresses have been rehearsing since January for the Competitive Class Plays.
  33|                           NEASC ACCREDITATION
  34|                           Lunenburg High School is currently going through the Self-Reflection portion of the
  35|                           accreditation process. Teachers, students, and parents completed an online survey which
  36|                           has given the school feedback on the various standards that we need to measure.
  37|                           Teachers have collected evidence and will make claims as to where the school is in
  38|                           reference to the various principles within each standard. The five standards are: Learning
  39|                           Culture, Student Learning, Professional Practices, Learning Support, and Learning
  40|                           Resources. In May, two to four individuals from NEASC will come out to do a
  41|                           collaborative conference. They will be presented with our self-reflection, along with
  42|                           recommendations and commendations. The school will then develop and implement a
  43|                                                                                              116

===PAGE 117=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|                       growth plan over the next two years. The final accreditation visit will be conducted in
   2|                       2020 and will look at how well we are making progress on our growth plan.
   3|                       SCHOOL/COMMUNITY PARTNERSHIPS
   4|                       Throughout the year, Lunenburg High School students are involved in a multitude of
   5|                       fundraising and collection endeavors. In the fall, a canned goods drive was held and
   6|                       admission to the PowderPuff game was two canned goods. Everything collected was
   7|                       donated to the Lunenburg Food Pantry. Around the holiday season, advisories collected
   8|                       items for the Lunenburg Gift Mart.
   9|                       There is a Senior English class running this year that has a community service learning
  10|                       component.           Students were tasked with                            researching and identifying needs in the
  11|                       community and then forming an action plan to address the needs. For example, we have
  12|                       students working on: a pet shelter drive, creating trail maps for Lunenburg conservation
  13|                       land, bringing a small breakfast program to LHS, and forming a group to inform and
  14|                       educate about mental health issues.
  15|                       Lunenburg High School received a generous donation in the amount of $500 from Mr.
  16|                       Mike's Mobil in Lunenburg, MA. The grant is to be used toward STEM initiatives at the
  17|                       high school. We are in the process of purchasing a new robot for our Robotics class.
  18|                       Hannaford Helps Schools made a generous donation to Lunenburg High School of $500
  19|                       from receipt sales. This money will be used to benefit a variety of programs at LHS.
  20|                       In March, the National Honor Society will be having its annual Blood Drive. Students
  21|                       sixteen and over (and staff members) will be able to participate in the Blood Drive with
  22|                       parent permission.
  23|                       LHS has held evening presentations for parents/guardians on the following topics:
  24|                             • Planning for High School (grade 8)
  25|                             • Early College Planning (grades 9-10)
  26|                            • Post-Secondary Education Planning (grade 11)
  27|                             • College Application Process (grade 12)
  28|                            • Financing your Education/Financial Aid (grades 8-12)
  29|                       LHS hosted 40 college admissions counselors in the school counseling office to visit with
  30|                       LHS students. In addition, we held two "Decision Day" programs with Fitchburg State
  31|                       University and Mount Wachusett Community College.
  32|                       MCAS RESULTS
  33|                        We are pleased to announce that Lunenburg High School is recognized by the
  34|                       Department of Elementary and Secondary Education as a Level 1 school. As a Level 1
  35|                       school, we continue to be among the highest performing schools in the Commonwealth.
  36|                       We are very proud of the work that our students and teachers have done to prepare for the
  37|                       MCAS test. While we are recognized as a high performing school, we understand this is
  38|                       only one measure used to show student growth and that there is constantly room for
  39|                       improvement
  40|                                                                                              117

   1|                                                Lunenburg High School - 2017 MCAS Results (Grade 10)
   2|                                     Subject Test                               Advanced/Proficient                          State Average (Adv/Pro)
   3|                                            ELA                                              93%                                              91%
   4|                                           Mathr                                             90%                                              79%
   5|                               Science/Technology                                            84%                                             74%
   6|                         When you combine the percentage bands of the advanced and proficient range, LHS tenth
   7|                         grade students scored 93% on the ELA test. In Mathematics, tenth grade students scored
   8|                         90% advanced/proficient. In Science/Technology/Engineering, tenth grade students
   9|                         scored 84% advanced/proficient. We are extremely proud of our students and teachers
  10|                         for their continued improvement, and we will work to move all students to the level of
  11|                         proficiency as measured by MCAS and our goal of college and career readiness as they
  12|                         move through our curriculum.
  13|                         We were also very pleased to learn that 29 seniors at Lunenburg High School received
  14|                         the state's John and Abigail Adams Scholarship this year. The scholarship is available to
  15|                         students whose MCAS performance puts them at the top 25 percent of their district. To
  16|                         be eligible, students must either score Advanced on one exam and Proficient on the other,
  17|                         or Advanced on both the English and Math exams.
  18|                         HOMECOMING, ATHLETICS AND EXTRACURRICULAR ACTIVITIES
  19|                         Homecoming was once again a great town-wide celebration, culminating with a beautiful
  20|                         parade complete with floats representing all four high school classes. Many of our sports
  21|                         teams qualified for tournament and district play. The Lunenburg High School Marching
  22|                         Band picked up second and third place at two competitions in the fall. The LHS Mock
  23|                         Trial team has performed well this year, going 2-1 in local trials.                                       The Latin Club, Jazz
  24|                         Band and the Math team represented LHS in numerous competitions; the 52nd annual
  25|                         Competitive Class Plays delighted the audience, and The Pops and Senior Showcase are
  26|                         scheduled for the spring.
  27|                         COMMUNITY BUILDING
  28|                         National Honor Society successfully supported the "Giftmart" drive during the holidays,
  29|                         helping to provide joy to needy children and families in conjunction with local agencies.
  30|                         The World Hunger Task Force will be raising money through Penny Wars this spring.
  31|                         The money will be donated toward the fight against hunger.
  32|                         In March, the National Honor Society will be running its annual Blood Drive. Students
  33|                         and staff participate in the drive. Our NHS students are looking to add a second date and
  34|                         expand the blood drive for Lunenburg community members.
  35|                         We started an Advisory program two years ago. One goal of Advisory is to have students
  36|                          have at least one adult who will get to know him/her over the course of four years.
  37|                         Students were randomly distributed into small grade-level groups. They will stay with
  38|                         the same group of students, as well. Another goal of Advisory is to develop
  39|                         relationships with students of different backgrounds and social groups so that students
  40|                         can get to know each other and develop an appreciation of the diversity that we do have
  41|                         in Lunenburg. Some Advisories will be facilitating some community service learning
  42|                         projects.

===PAGE 122=== (ocr, 47 lines)
   1|                          The 2016-2017 school year marked my third year as Superintendent-Director of Montachusett Regional
   2|                         Vocational Technical School District, and in my short tenure here, 1 am proud to say I have seen the school
   3|                         reach new levels of academic excellence, technical proficiency, and student engagement. Perhaps the most
   4|                         exciting aspect of my role here is bearing witness to the creativity and innovation at work everyday in our
   5|                         classrooms and shops. Our students and instructors work collaboratively to explore scientific concepts,
   6|                         designing experiments that are conducted aboard the International Space Station; they study automation
   7|                         and additive manufacturing through the use of robotic arms and 3-D printers; and even build new
   8|                         educational facilities that students will grow and learn in for years to come. Monty Tech is a school unlike
   9|                         any other and I am honored to serve the students, faculty and staff here.
  10|                         In many respects, 2016-2017 was a banner year                        at Monty Tech. We launched a whole school 1:1
  11|                          Chromebook initiative, providing all students with increased access to instructional technology, and
  12|                         improving communications between teachers and students beyond the traditional school day. We received
  13|                         more than $645,000 in competitive grants, and have been able to provide students with remarkable field
  14|                         trips to connect their learning to STEM career pathways that spark imagination and creativity. We
  15|                         provided high quality professional development opportunities to support the school's new biomedical
  16|                         courses, bringing innovative new science coursework and curriculum to students with an interest in
  17|                         biomedical and biotechnology career pathways. Construction of the Monty Tech Veterinary Science
  18|                          Training Center and Community Clinic continued throughout 2016-2017, providing tremendous hands-on
  19|                         learning opportunities to students in our Carpentry, Plumbing, Electrical, Cabinetmaking, HVAC,
  20|                          Masonry, and Welding trades. School administrators launched an unprecedented fundraising campaign,
  21|                         and successfully raised more than $2.5M in donations and in-kind matching services to support this
  22|                         project. And finally, thanks to an extremely generous Skills Capital Grant, we were able to renovate the
  23|                         Machine Technology shop, and purchase state-of-the-art machinery for students to train on, earning more
  24|                         industry recognized credentials than ever before.
  25|                         We recognize that students attending Monty Tech have chosen a high school experience that, to many, may
  26|                         seem complex and even daunting. Our students are required to pass the same statewide standardized tests
  27|                         as students in a comprehensive high school. They take English, mathematics, science, social studies and
  28|                         foreign language courses, just as high school students in your community might. A Monty Tech student,
  29|                         though, must also balance all of that with a rigorous vocational-technical education, specializing in one of
  30|                         our twenty programs. For four years our students work and learn in vocational settings that replicate
  31|                         industry standards. They train on the latest equipment, and, through the school's popular Co-operative
  32|                         Education Program, our students may even spend time working and learning in area businesses during their
  33|                         Junior and Senior year. It is a great responsibility to provide each and every student with high-quality,
  34|                         vocational-technical instruction, and we are honored to serve approximately 1,400 students each year.
  35|                         This work is only possible because of our talented educators and administrators, who remain focused on
  36|                         delivering the highest quality academic and vocational-technical education possible. Working closely with
  37|                         post-secondary colleagues, we aim to ensure our students graduate prepared to enter college pathways of
  38|                         their choosing. Similarly, relying on our expansive network of industry partners, we strive to improve upon
  39|                         each of our vocational programs to ensure that every graduate is not only prepared for entry-level careers
  40|                         in related pathways, but are also creative, independent thinkers, capable of solving complex, real-world
  41|                         situations that present themselves in today's workplaces. On their behalf, I am delighted to present the
  42|                         District's 2016-2017 annual report to you, providing a snapshot of the wonderful experiences happening on
  43|                         a day-to-day basis here at Monty Tech.
  44|                                                                                                                                       Respectfully submitted,
  45|                                                                                                                              Sheila M Marity
  46|                                                                                                      Sheila M. Harrity, Ed.D., Superintendent-Direckor
  47|                                                                                              122

   2|                                    Every student will graduate from Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School with the skills,
   3|                                    knowledge, and abilities to be a productive and effective member of an ever-changing society.
   4|                                                                                              Our District
   5|                                    Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School is a four-year career and technical high school
   6|                                    serving the member towns of:

  15|                                    administrators whose varied educational backgrounds, professional experiences, and areas of expertise

  17|                                    Superintendent and Principal, the team has transformed the school into one of the most sought- after high
  18|                                    schools in North Central Massachusetts.

   9|                            Throughout 2016-2017, Monty Tech offered a variety of opportunities for students, parents, and
  10|                            community members to learn about and visit the school. In October 2016, approximately 700 dis-
  11|                            trict eighth graders participated in the annual "Tour Day" event. Students toured our twenty voca-
  12|                            tional- technical areas and learned about the school's challenging academic offerings and exciting
  13|                            athletic and extracurricular programs. Career Awareness Night offered interested students the op-
  14|                            portunity to return in the evening with their family members to further explore the facilities and
  15|                            speak with staff members.
  16|                            The Vocational Interest Program (V.I.P.) offers area seventh and eighth grade students the chance
  17|                            to visit Monty Tech after school and participate in hands-on learning experiences across a variety
  18|                            of vocational/technical areas. The program continued to attract a large number of students during
  19|                            the 2016- 2017 school year, serving approximately 600 area students.
  20|                          Class of 2017 Awards
  21|                            Members of the Class of 2017 were awarded approximately $88,950 in scholarships. The Monty
  22|                            Tech Foundation generously provided $45,700 in scholarships to graduating seniors, ranging in
  23|                            amounts of $200 to $2,000. The Foundation also awarded $4,000 to the Practical Nursing gradu-
  24|                            ates. Once again, local and state organizations, as well as generous individuals, continue to recog-
  25|                            nize the ability and potential of Monty Tech graduates in the form of financial donations. The
  26|                            School Committee, administration, faculty, and graduates themselves, are grateful for this support.
  27|                            Articulation Agreements with local colleges also play an important role in helping reduce the cost
  28|                            of higher education. Qualified Monty Tech students are eligible to receive college credits through
  29|                            a number of articulation agreements with public and private colleges across the country. Approxi-
  30|                            mately 51% of the graduating class of 2017 reported plans to enroll at a 4-year college/university
  31|                            upon graduation. By earning college credits while still in high school, these students will save both
  32|                            time and money as they pursue advanced educational programs.
  33|                          Financial Report
  34|                            A great deal of effort was put forth by the School Committee, administration, and staff to develop a
  35|                            cost- effective budget for the fiscal year 2016-2017. The final fiscal year 2016-2017 Educational
  36|                            Plan totaled $26,374,468, which represents a .6% increase over the 2015-2016 Educational Plan.
  37|                            The District's FY17 budget exceeds the minimum spending required by Massachusetts General
  38|                            Law Chapter 70 by only $45,000 or .2%.

   1|                         The District was audited in October 2017 as part of the yearly financial audit by the accounting firm of
   2|                         Melanson, Heath and Co. from Greenfield, MA, and received a very good report.
   3|                                                                                 Grants and Contracts
   4|                         Monty Tech continues to pursue grant funding on an annual basis. These funds help provide many
   5|                         educational and social services to the student population. For fiscal year 2017, state and federal grant
   6|                         sources provided the school with $961,346. Programs funded by these grants include: Essential Health
   7|                         Services, Social Intervention and Mediation, Improving Teacher Quality, Special Education Services and
   8|                         Program Improvement, Title I Support, Perkins Occupational Education, and Marine Corp Junior Reserve
   9|                         Officer Training. The District also received more than $645,000 in competitive grants, including a Skills
  10|                         Capital Grant for $435,857 to purchase equipment for the Machine Technology Program, and a grant from
  11|                         the Massachusetts Life Sciences Center in the amount of $99,697 to expand the school's science program
  12|                        to include biomedical coursework and learning laboratories. Using these allocation and competitive funds,
  13|                        the school was able to purchase a variety of instructional technology, equipment, and supplies to enhance
  14|                        the learning experience across both academic and vocational programs.

  18|                        and Biology. For Spring 2017, Monty Tech's passing rate on the English Language Arts was 100%,
